Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Two bears are playing tic-tac-toe via mail. It's boring for them to play usual tic-tac-toe game, so they are a playing modified version of this game. Here are its rules.

The game is played on the following field.

<image>

Players are making moves by turns. At first move a player can put his chip in any cell of any small field. For following moves, there are some restrictions: if during last move the opposite player put his chip to cell with coordinates (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move should be done in one of the cells of the small field with coordinates (xl, yl). For example, if in the first move a player puts his chip to lower left cell of central field, then the second player on his next move should put his chip into some cell of lower left field (pay attention to the first test case). If there are no free cells in the required field, the player can put his chip to any empty cell on any field.

You are given current state of the game and coordinates of cell in which the last move was done. You should find all cells in which the current player can put his chip.

A hare works as a postman in the forest, he likes to foul bears. Sometimes he changes the game field a bit, so the current state of the game could be unreachable. However, after his changes the cell where the last move was done is not empty. You don't need to find if the state is unreachable or not, just output possible next moves according to the rules.

Input

First 11 lines contains descriptions of table with 9 rows and 9 columns which are divided into 9 small fields by spaces and empty lines. Each small field is described by 9 characters without spaces and empty lines. character "x" (ASCII-code 120) means that the cell is occupied with chip of the first player, character "o" (ASCII-code 111) denotes a field occupied with chip of the second player, character "." (ASCII-code 46) describes empty cell.

The line after the table contains two integers x and y (1 ≤ x, y ≤ 9). They describe coordinates of the cell in table where the last move was done. Rows in the table are numbered from up to down and columns are numbered from left to right.

It's guaranteed that cell where the last move was done is filled with "x" or "o". Also, it's guaranteed that there is at least one empty cell. It's not guaranteed that current state of game is reachable.

Output

Output the field in same format with characters "!" (ASCII-code 33) on positions where the current player can put his chip. All other cells should not be modified.

Examples

Input

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... x.. ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...
6 4


Output

... ... ... 
... ... ... 
... ... ... 

... ... ... 
... ... ... 
... x.. ... 

!!! ... ... 
!!! ... ... 
!!! ... ... 



Input

xoo x.. x..
ooo ... ...
ooo ... ...

x.. x.. x..
... ... ...
... ... ...

x.. x.. x..
... ... ...
... ... ...
7 4


Output

xoo x!! x!! 
ooo !!! !!! 
ooo !!! !!! 

x!! x!! x!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 

x!! x!! x!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 



Input

o.. ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...

... xxx ...
... xox ...
... ooo ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...
5 5


Output

o!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 

!!! xxx !!! 
!!! xox !!! 
!!! ooo !!! 

!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 

Note

In the first test case the first player made a move to lower left cell of central field, so the second player can put a chip only to cells of lower left field.

In the second test case the last move was done to upper left cell of lower central field, however all cells in upper left field are occupied, so the second player can put his chip to any empty cell.

In the third test case the last move was done to central cell of central field, so current player can put his chip to any cell of central field, which is already occupied, so he can move anywhere. Pay attention that this state of the game is unreachable.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9x9 grid divided into 9 smaller 3x3 fields
2. The game has special rules for moves:
   - First move can be anywhere
   - After a move to cell (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move must be in the small field at position (xl, yl)
   - If there are no free cells in the required field, the player can move anywhere
3. We need to find all valid cells where the current player can place their chip

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 11 lines of input (9 lines of fields separated by empty lines)
- Extract the 9 small 3x3 fields
- Parse the last move coordinates (x, y)

### Step 2: Understand field positioning
- The 9 small fields are arranged in a 3x3 grid
- Field at position (i, j) means:
  - Row i: 3i to 3i+2 (0-based)
  - Column j: 3j to 3j+2 (0-based)
- The last move (x, y) tells us which small field we need to "follow"

### Step 3: Determine valid moves
- Find which small field the last move was in
- If that field has free cells, the current player must move in that field
- If that field has no free cells, the current player can move anywhere

### Step 4: Implementation

```python
# Read the input
lines = []
for _ in range(11):
    lines.append(input().strip())

# Parse the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-based indexing
x -= 1
y -= 1

# The 9 fields are divided as follows:
# Fields are at positions (i, j) where i, j in [0, 2]
# The cell (x, y) belongs to field at (x // 3, y // 3)

field_x = x // 3
field_y = y // 3

# Create a 3x3 grid of 3x3 fields
fields = []
current_line = 0
for i in range(3):
    field_row = []
    for j in range(3):
        # Extract the 3x3 field at position (i, j)
        field = []
        for k in range(3):
            row = lines[current_line + k].split()
            field.append(row[j * 3:(j + 1) * 3])
        field_row.append(field)
    fields.append(field_row)
    current_line += 3

# Find which field the last move was in
# The last move was at (x, y) which belongs to field (field_x, field_y)

# Determine if there are free cells in the target field
target_field_has_free_cells = False
for row in fields[field_x][field_y]:
    for cell in row:
        if cell.count('.') > 0:
            target_field_has_free_cells = True
            break
    if target_field_has_free_cells:
        break

# If there are free cells in the target field, we can only move in that field
# Otherwise, we can move anywhere

# Create result grid
result_lines = []
for i in range(9):
    result_line = []
    for j in range(9):
        # Find which field this position belongs to
        field_i = i // 3
        field_j = j // 3
        
        # Check if this is the target field
        if field_i == field_x and field_j == field_y:
            # We can only move in this field if it has free cells
            # But we need to check if the cell is currently empty
            if fields[field_i][field_j][i % 3][j % 3] == '.':
                result_line.append('!')
            else:
                result_line.append(fields[field_i][field_j][i % 3][j % 3])
        else:
            # We can move anywhere if the target field has no free cells
            # Or if we're not in the target field
            if fields[field_i][field_j][i % 3][j % 3] == '.':
                result_line.append('!')
            else:
                result_line.append(fields[field_i][field_j][i % 3][j % 3])
    
    result_lines.append(' '.join(result_line))

# Output the result
for line in result_lines:
    print(line)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9x9 grid divided into 9 smaller 3x3 fields
2. Players take turns placing chips (x or o)
3. After the first move, there's a restriction: if the opponent placed a chip at position (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move must be in the small field at coordinates (xl, yl)
4. If all cells in the required field are occupied, the player can place in any empty cell
5. We need to find all possible next moves for the current player

## Step-by-step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 11 lines (9 lines of fields with spaces, 2 empty lines)
- Extract the 9 small 3x3 fields
- Get the coordinates (x, y) of the last move

### Step 2: Determine the small field of the last move
- The coordinates go from 1-9, so we need to convert to 0-8
- The small field is determined by (x-1)//3, (y-1)//3 (0-2 for each dimension)

### Step 3: Find possible moves
- If the required small field has empty cells, the current player must place in that field
- If the required small field has no empty cells, the player can place in any empty cell

### Step 4: Output the result
- Replace empty cells (".") with "!" for valid moves
- Keep original characters for occupied cells

## Implementation

```python
# Read the 9x9 grid
grid = []
for _ in range(9):
    line = input().strip()
    grid.append(line)

# Read the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-based indexing
x_idx = x - 1
y_idx = y - 1

# Find which small field the last move was in
small_field_x = x_idx // 3
small_field_y = y_idx // 3

# Determine the target small field for next move
# The next move must be in the small field at (small_field_x, small_field_y)
target_small_x = small_field_x
target_small_y = small_field_y

# Create result grid
result = []
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        # Determine which small field this cell belongs to
        small_x = i // 3
        small_y = j // 3
        
        # If this cell is in the target small field, it's a valid move
        # Otherwise, only valid if it's empty in other fields or if target field is full
        if small_x == target_small_x and small_y == target_small_y:
            # Check if this cell is currently empty
            if grid[i][j] == '.':
                row.append('!')
            else:
                row.append(grid[i][j])
        else:
            # If not in target field, can only place here if target field is full
            # But since we're checking validity, we need to see if target field has any empty cells
            
            # Actually, we need to re-think: the rule is that the next move must be in the field
            # that corresponds to the last move's position. So if the last move was in field (sx, sy),
            # the next move must be in field (sx, sy) if it has empty cells.
            # If it doesn't, then any empty cell is valid.
            
            # So we need to check if the target field has any empty cells
            # But we're processing cell by cell, so we can't decide yet.
            
            # Let's handle this differently: first determine if the target field has empty cells
            # If it does, then only cells in that field are valid (if empty)
            # If it doesn't, then all empty cells are valid
            
            # We'll calculate this at the end
            pass
    
    result.append(''.join(row))

# We need to determine if the target field has any empty cells
target_field_has_empty = False
target_field_x = small_field_x
target_field_y = small_field_y

# Check if the target small field has any empty cells
for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        row = 3 * target_field_x + i
        col = 3 * target_field_y + j
        if grid[row][col] == '.':
            target_field_has_empty = True
            break
    if target_field_has_empty:
        break

# Now, for each cell, determine if it's a valid move
final_result = []
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        # Determine which small field this cell belongs to
        small_x = i // 3
        small_y = j // 3
        
        # If this cell is in the target small field
        if small_x == target_field_x and small_y == target_field_y:
            # If target field has empty cells, then this cell is valid only if empty
            if grid[i][j] == '.':
                row.append('!')
            else:
                row.append(grid[i][j])
        else:
            # If target field has no empty cells, we can place anywhere in empty cells
            # If target field has empty cells, we can only place in the target field
            if target_field_has_empty:
                # Only valid in the target field
                row.append(grid[i][j])
            else:
                # No restrictions - can place in any empty cell
                if grid[i][j] == '.':
                    row.append('!')
                else:
                    row.append(grid[i][j])
    
    final_result.append(''.join(row))

# Output the result
for row in final_result:
    print(row)
```

Wait, I see a flaw in my logic. Let me reconsider the problem:

The key insight is that after the first move, if the last move was at (xl, yl), then the next move must be in the small field at (xl, yl). If that field has no empty cells, then any empty cell can be chosen.

So I need to:
1. Find which small field the last move was in
2. Check if that small field has any empty cells
3. For each cell:
   - If it's in that small field and empty, mark as "!"
   - If it's not in that small field and empty, mark as "!" only if the target field has no empty cells
   - Otherwise keep the original character

```python
# Read the 9x9 grid
grid = []
for _ in range(9):
    line = input().strip()
    grid.append(line)

# Read the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-based indexing
x_idx = x - 1
y_idx = y - 1

# Find which small field the last move was in
small_field_x = x_idx // 3
small_field_y = y_idx // 3

# Determine the target small field for next move
target_small_x = small_field_x
target_small_y = small_field_y

# Check if the target small field has any empty cells
target_field_has_empty = False
for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        row = 3 * target_small_x + i
        col = 3 * target_small_y + j
        if grid[row][col] == '.':
            target_field_has_empty = True
            break
    if target_field_has_empty:
        break

# Create result
result = []
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        # Determine which small field this cell belongs to
        small_x = i // 3
        small_y = j // 3
        
        # If this cell is in the target small field
        if small_x == target_small_x and small_y == target_small_y:
            # If the target field has empty cells, we can only place here if empty
            if grid[i][j] == '.':
                row.append('!')
            else:
                row.append(grid[i][j])
        else:
            # If target field has no empty cells, we can place anywhere
            # If target field has empty cells, we cannot place here
            if not target_field_has_empty:
                # Can place in any empty cell
                if grid[i][j] == '.':
                    row.append('!')
                else:
                    row.append(grid[i][j])
            else:
                # Cannot place here since we must go to the target field
                row.append(grid[i][j])
    
    result.append(''.join(row))

# Output the result
for row in result:
    print(row)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Two bears are playing tic-tac-toe via mail. It's boring for them to play usual tic-tac-toe game, so they are a playing modified version of this game. Here are its rules.

The game is played on the following field.

<image>

Players are making moves by turns. At first move a player can put his chip in any cell of any small field. For following moves, there are some restrictions: if during last move the opposite player put his chip to cell with coordinates (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move should be done in one of the cells of the small field with coordinates (xl, yl). For example, if in the first move a player puts his chip to lower left cell of central field, then the second player on his next move should put his chip into some cell of lower left field (pay attention to the first test case). If there are no free cells in the required field, the player can put his chip to any empty cell on any field.

You are given current state of the game and coordinates of cell in which the last move was done. You should find all cells in which the current player can put his chip.

A hare works as a postman in the forest, he likes to foul bears. Sometimes he changes the game field a bit, so the current state of the game could be unreachable. However, after his changes the cell where the last move was done is not empty. You don't need to find if the state is unreachable or not, just output possible next moves according to the rules.

Input

First 11 lines contains descriptions of table with 9 rows and 9 columns which are divided into 9 small fields by spaces and empty lines. Each small field is described by 9 characters without spaces and empty lines. character "x" (ASCII-code 120) means that the cell is occupied with chip of the first player, character "o" (ASCII-code 111) denotes a field occupied with chip of the second player, character "." (ASCII-code 46) describes empty cell.

The line after the table contains two integers x and y (1 ≤ x, y ≤ 9). They describe coordinates of the cell in table where the last move was done. Rows in the table are numbered from up to down and columns are numbered from left to right.

It's guaranteed that cell where the last move was done is filled with "x" or "o". Also, it's guaranteed that there is at least one empty cell. It's not guaranteed that current state of game is reachable.

Output

Output the field in same format with characters "!" (ASCII-code 33) on positions where the current player can put his chip. All other cells should not be modified.

Examples

Input

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... x.. ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...
6 4


Output

... ... ... 
... ... ... 
... ... ... 

... ... ... 
... ... ... 
... x.. ... 

!!! ... ... 
!!! ... ... 
!!! ... ... 



Input

xoo x.. x..
ooo ... ...
ooo ... ...

x.. x.. x..
... ... ...
... ... ...

x.. x.. x..
... ... ...
... ... ...
7 4


Output

xoo x!! x!! 
ooo !!! !!! 
ooo !!! !!! 

x!! x!! x!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 

x!! x!! x!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 



Input

o.. ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...

... xxx ...
... xox ...
... ooo ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...
5 5


Output

o!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 

!!! xxx !!! 
!!! xox !!! 
!!! ooo !!! 

!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 

Note

In the first test case the first player made a move to lower left cell of central field, so the second player can put a chip only to cells of lower left field.

In the second test case the last move was done to upper left cell of lower central field, however all cells in upper left field are occupied, so the second player can put his chip to any empty cell.

In the third test case the last move was done to central cell of central field, so current player can put his chip to any cell of central field, which is already occupied, so he can move anywhere. Pay attention that this state of the game is unreachable.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9×9 tic-tac-toe board divided into 9 smaller 3×3 fields
2. Players take turns making moves
3. The first move can be anywhere
4. After that, if the opponent made a move at position (x, y) in some small field, the next player must make a move in the small field that contains (x, y)
5. If there are no empty cells in the required field, the player can move anywhere
6. We need to find all valid next moves based on the last move's position

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 9 lines of the board (with spaces between fields)
- Parse the 9×9 grid into 9 separate 3×3 fields
- Read the last move coordinates (x, y)

### Step 2: Determine which small field contains the last move
- The board is divided into 9 fields: (1,1) to (3,3) where each field is 3×3
- For a position (x, y), it belongs to field (i, j) where:
  - i = (x-1)//3 + 1 (row index of field, 1-based)
  - j = (y-1)//3 + 1 (column index of field, 1-based)

### Step 3: Find valid next moves
- If the field containing the last move has any empty cells, next player can only move in that field
- If all cells in that field are occupied, next player can move anywhere
- Mark empty cells with "!" in the output

### Step 4: Handle the output
- Keep the original board values for non-empty cells
- Replace empty cells (".") that are valid moves with "!"
- Print the board in the same format

## Implementation

```python
# Read the board
board = []
for _ in range(9):
    line = input().strip()
    board.append(line)

# Read last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-based indexing
x -= 1
y -= 1

# Determine which small field contains the last move
# Each small field is 3x3, so we divide by 3
field_x = x // 3
field_y = y // 3

# Find the specific position within the field
pos_in_field_x = x % 3
pos_in_field_y = y % 3

# Create a copy of the board for output
result = []
for i in range(9):
    result.append(list(board[i]))

# Determine if we need to restrict moves
# First, find which field the last move was in
last_field_x = (x // 3)  # 0-indexed
last_field_y = (y // 3)  # 0-indexed

# Check if the target field is empty - if not, we need to restrict moves to that field
# But we need to check if there are any empty cells in the target field
target_field_has_empty = False

# Check if the target field has any empty cells
for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        row = last_field_x * 3 + i
        col = last_field_y * 3 + j
        if board[row][col] == '.':
            target_field_has_empty = True
            break
    if target_field_has_empty:
        break

# If the target field has empty cells, next move must be in that field
# Otherwise, next move can be anywhere
if target_field_has_empty:
    # We can only move in the field where the last move was made
    for i in range(3):
        for j in range(3):
            row = last_field_x * 3 + i
            col = last_field_y * 3 + j
            if board[row][col] == '.':
                result[row][col] = '!'
else:
    # We can move anywhere (since the field is full)
    for i in range(9):
        for j in range(9):
            if board[i][j] == '.':
                result[i][j] = '!'

# Print the result
for row in result:
    print(' '.join(row))
```

Wait, I need to reconsider the logic. Let me trace through the examples:

In the first example:
- Last move at (6,4) which is row 5, col 3 (0-indexed)
- This is in field (5//3, 3//3) = (1,1) - the center field
- The center field has a chip at (5,3) which is the lower left cell
- So the next player must move in the field that contains (5,3), which is field (1,1)
- Field (1,1) has positions: (3,0), (3,1), (3,2), (4,0), (4,1), (4,2), (5,0), (5,1), (5,2)
- The center cell (5,3) is at (5,3) which is in field (1,1), column 1
- So the next move should be in the lower left field of the center field? 

I think I misunderstood. Let me re-read:

"If during last move the opposite player put his chip to cell with coordinates (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move should be done in one of the cells of the small field with coordinates (xl, yl)"

So if the last move was at (x,y), the next move must be in the small field that has coordinates (x,y).

The coordinates (x,y) refer to the position in the 9x9 grid, and we need to find which 3x3 field this belongs to.

The field coordinates are:
- Field (0,0): rows 0-2, cols 0-2
- Field (0,1): rows 0-2, cols 3-5  
- Field (0,2): rows 0-2, cols 6-8
- Field (1,0): rows 3-5, cols 0-2
- Field (1,1): rows 3-5, cols 3-5
- Field (1,2): rows 3-5, cols 6-8
- Field (2,0): rows 6-8, cols 0-2
- Field (2,1): rows 6-8, cols 3-5
- Field (2,2): rows 6-8, cols 6-8

So for position (6,4): 
- x = 6, y = 4
- field_x = 6 // 3 = 2
- field_y = 4 // 3 = 1

So the move was in field (2,1). The next move must be in field (2,1).

Let me correct the solution:

```python
# Read the board
board_lines = []
for _ in range(9):
    line = input().strip()
    board_lines.append(line)

# Read last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-based indexing
x -= 1
y -= 1

# Determine which small field contains the last move
field_x = x // 3  # 0-indexed field row
field_y = y // 3  # 0-indexed field column

# Check if the target field has any empty cells
target_field_has_empty = False
for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        row = field_x * 3 + i
        col = field_y * 3 + j
        if board_lines[row][col] == '.':
            target_field_has_empty = True
            break
    if target_field_has_empty:
        break

# Create result board
result = []
for i in range(9):
    result.append(list(board_lines[i]))

# Determine valid moves
if target_field_has_empty:
    # Next move must be in the field where the last move was made
    for i in range(3):
        for j in range(3):
            row = field_x * 3 + i
            col = field_y * 3 + j
            if board_lines[row][col] == '.':
                result[row][col] = '!'
else:
    # All cells in the target field are occupied, so can move anywhere
    for i in range(9):
        for j in range(9):
            if board_lines[i][j] == '.':
                result[i][j] = '!'

# Print the result
for row in result:
    print(' '.join(row))
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9×9 grid divided into 9 smaller 3×3 fields
2. Players take turns placing chips ('x' or 'o')
3. The key rule: if the last move was at position (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move must be in the small field at position (xl, yl)
4. If that small field has no empty cells, the player can place in any empty cell
5. We need to find all possible next moves for the current player

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input structure
- Read 11 lines of input (9 lines of 3x3 fields and 2 lines of separation)
- The fields are separated by spaces and empty lines
- Extract the 9 small fields

### Step 2: Understand coordinates
- The 9×9 grid has coordinates from 1-9 (1-indexed)
- Each small field is 3×3, so we need to convert (x,y) to field coordinates
- Field coordinates: (field_row, field_col) where field_row = (x-1)//3, field_col = (y-1)//3
- Within field: (row, col) = (x-1) % 3, (y-1) % 3

### Step 3: Determine possible moves
1. Find which small field was just played (based on last move coordinates)
2. If that field has empty cells, the next move must be in that field
3. If that field has no empty cells, the next move can be in any empty cell

### Step 4: Apply the rule
- Find the field that contains the last move
- Check if there are empty cells in that field
- For each empty cell in that field (if any), mark it with '!'
- If no empty cells in that field, mark all empty cells with '!' (meaning current player can move anywhere)

### Step 5: Output the result

```python
# Read the 11 lines of input
lines = []
for _ in range(11):
    line = input().strip()
    lines.append(line)

# Parse the 9 small fields
fields = []
current_field = []
for i in range(11):
    line = lines[i]
    if line:
        current_field.append(line)
    else:
        if current_field:
            fields.append(current_field)
            current_field = []

if current_field:
    fields.append(current_field)

# Extract the last move coordinates
x_last, y_last = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-indexed coordinates
x_last -= 1
y_last -= 1

# Find which small field the last move was in
field_row = x_last // 3
field_col = y_last // 3

# Find which position within the field
row_in_field = x_last % 3
col_in_field = y_last % 3

# Create result fields
result_fields = []
for field in fields:
    result_field = []
    for row in field:
        result_field.append(list(row))
    result_fields.append(result_field)

# Determine possible moves
# The next move must be in the field that contains the last move (field_row, field_col)
field_has_empty = False
for r in range(3):
    for c in range(3):
        if fields[field_row][field_col][r * 3 + c] == '.':
            field_has_empty = True
            break
    if field_has_empty:
        break

# If the field has empty cells, next move must be in this field
# If the field has no empty cells, next move can be anywhere
if field_has_empty:
    # Next move must be in field (field_row, field_col)
    for r in range(3):
        for c in range(3):
            field_idx = field_row * 3 + field_col
            cell_idx = r * 3 + c
            if fields[field_row][field_col][cell_idx] == '.':
                result_fields[field_idx][r][c] = '!'
else:
    # Next move can be in any empty cell
    for field_idx in range(9):
        for r in range(3):
            for c in range(3):
                cell_idx = r * 3 + c
                if fields[field_idx][cell_idx] == '.':
                    result_fields[field_idx][r][c] = '!'

# Convert result back to string format
output_lines = []
for field in result_fields:
    output_lines.append(" ".join("".join(row) for row in field))

for line in output_lines:
    print(line)
```

# Read the 11 lines of input
lines = []
for _ in range(11):
    line = input().strip()
    lines.append(line)

# Parse the 9 small fields
fields = []
current_field = []
for i in range(11):
    line = lines[i]
    if line:
        current_field.append(line)
    else:
        if current_field:
            fields.append(current_field)
            current_field = []

if current_field:
    fields.append(current_field)

# Extract the last move coordinates
x_last, y_last = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-indexed coordinates
x_last -= 1
y_last -= 1

# Find which small field the last move was in
field_row = x_last // 3
field_col = y_last // 3

# Find which position within the field
row_in_field = x_last % 3
col_in_field = y_last % 3

# Create result fields
result_fields = []
for field in fields:
    result_field = []
    for row in field:
        result_field.append(list(row))
    result_fields.append(result_field)

# Determine possible moves
# The next move must be in the field that contains the last move (field_row, field_col)
field_has_empty = False
for r in range(3):
    for c in range(3):
        if fields[field_row][field_col][r * 3 + c] == '.':
            field_has_empty = True
            break
    if field_has_empty:
        break

# If the field has empty cells, next move must be in this field
# If the field has no empty cells, next move can be anywhere
if field_has_empty:
    # Next move must be in field (field_row, field_col)
    for r in range(3):
        for c in range(3):
            field_idx = field_row * 3 + field_col
            cell_idx = r * 3 + c
            if fields[field_row][field_col][cell_idx] == '.':
                result_fields[field_idx][r][c] = '!'
else:
    # Next move can be in any empty cell
    for field_idx in range(9):
        for r in range(3):
            for c in range(3):
                cell_idx = r * 3 + c
                if fields[field_idx][cell_idx] == '.':
                    result_fields[field_idx][r][c] = '!'

# Convert result back to string format
output_lines = []
for field in result_fields:
    output_lines.append(" ".join("".join(row) for row in field))

for line in output_lines:
    print(line)

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9x9 grid divided into 9 smaller 3x3 fields (rows 1-3, 4-6, 7-9 and columns 1-3, 4-6, 7-9)
2. The game has specific move rules:
   - First move: can go to any cell
   - Subsequent moves: if the last move was at (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move must be in the small field at (xl, yl)
   - If there are no free cells in the required field, the player can move to any empty cell
3. We're given the current state and the coordinates of the last move
4. We need to find all possible cells where the current player can move

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 11 lines (9 for the grid, 1 for coordinates)
- Parse the 9x9 grid into 9 smaller 3x3 fields
- Extract the coordinates of the last move (1-indexed)

### Step 2: Determine the current field constraints
- Find which small field the last move was in
- If that small field has no empty cells, the current player can move anywhere
- Otherwise, the current player can only move in that specific small field

### Step 3: Apply the rules
- For each empty cell in the grid:
  - If the last move was in field (i,j), check if that field has any empty cells
  - If the field has no empty cells, mark the cell as possible (with "!")
  - If the field has empty cells, only mark cells within that field as possible

### Step 4: Output the result
- Print the modified grid with "!" at valid move positions

## Implementation

```python
                # This cell is in the target field
                row_result += "!"
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Two bears are playing tic-tac-toe via mail. It's boring for them to play usual tic-tac-toe game, so they are a playing modified version of this game. Here are its rules.

The game is played on the following field.

<image>

Players are making moves by turns. At first move a player can put his chip in any cell of any small field. For following moves, there are some restrictions: if during last move the opposite player put his chip to cell with coordinates (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move should be done in one of the cells of the small field with coordinates (xl, yl). For example, if in the first move a player puts his chip to lower left cell of central field, then the second player on his next move should put his chip into some cell of lower left field (pay attention to the first test case). If there are no free cells in the required field, the player can put his chip to any empty cell on any field.

You are given current state of the game and coordinates of cell in which the last move was done. You should find all cells in which the current player can put his chip.

A hare works as a postman in the forest, he likes to foul bears. Sometimes he changes the game field a bit, so the current state of the game could be unreachable. However, after his changes the cell where the last move was done is not empty. You don't need to find if the state is unreachable or not, just output possible next moves according to the rules.

Input

First 11 lines contains descriptions of table with 9 rows and 9 columns which are divided into 9 small fields by spaces and empty lines. Each small field is described by 9 characters without spaces and empty lines. character "x" (ASCII-code 120) means that the cell is occupied with chip of the first player, character "o" (ASCII-code 111) denotes a field occupied with chip of the second player, character "." (ASCII-code 46) describes empty cell.

The line after the table contains two integers x and y (1 ≤ x, y ≤ 9). They describe coordinates of the cell in table where the last move was done. Rows in the table are numbered from up to down and columns are numbered from left to right.

It's guaranteed that cell where the last move was done is filled with "x" or "o". Also, it's guaranteed that there is at least one empty cell. It's not guaranteed that current state of game is reachable.

Output

Output the field in same format with characters "!" (ASCII-code 33) on positions where the current player can put his chip. All other cells should not be modified.

Examples

Input

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... x.. ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...
6 4


Output

... ... ... 
... ... ... 
... ... ... 

... ... ... 
... ... ... 
... x.. ... 

!!! ... ... 
!!! ... ... 
!!! ... ... 



Input

xoo x.. x..
ooo ... ...
ooo ... ...

x.. x.. x..
... ... ...
... ... ...

x.. x.. x..
... ... ...
... ... ...
7 4


Output

xoo x!! x!! 
ooo !!! !!! 
ooo !!! !!! 

x!! x!! x!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!! 

x!! x!! x!! 
!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!!

o.. ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...

... xxx ...
... xox ...
... ooo ...

... ... ...
... ... ...

!!! ooo !!!

In the second test case the last move was done to upper left cell of lower central field, however all cells in upper left field are occupied, so the second player can put his chip to any empty cell.

In the third test case the last move was done to central cell of central field, so current player can put his chip to any cell of central field, which is already occupied, so he can move anywhere. Pay attention that this state of the game is unreachable.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9x9 grid divided into 9 smaller 3x3 fields
2. Players take turns, with special rules for moves after the first move
3. After the first move, if a player places a chip at position (xl, yl) in some small field, the next player must place their chip in the same small field that contains (xl, yl)
4. If the required field has no empty cells, the player can place in any empty cell
5. We need to output the current possible moves with "!" for valid positions

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input and understand the board structure
- The 9x9 grid is divided into 9 small 3x3 fields
- Each field is identified by its position (0-2, 0-2) in the 3x3 grid of fields
- Coordinates given (x, y) are 1-indexed, so we need to convert to 0-indexed

### Step 2: Determine the current position and constraints
- Find which small field contains the last move position (x, y)
- This will determine the restricted field for the next move

### Step 3: Apply the rules
- If the restricted field has empty cells, the next move must be in that field
- If the restricted field has no empty cells, the next move can be anywhere
- Mark all valid positions with "!"

### Step 4: Handle the output formatting
- Keep the original grid structure with spaces between fields
- Replace empty cells with "!" for valid moves

## Implementation

```python
# Read the input
lines = []
for _ in range(11):
    line = input().strip()
    lines.append(line)

# Parse the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-indexed
x -= 1
y -= 1

# Determine which small field contains the last move
# Each small field is 3x3, so divide by 3
field_x = x // 3
field_y = y // 3

# Convert the grid to a 2D array for easier manipulation
grid = []
for i in range(9):
    row = lines[i].split(' ')
    grid.append(row)

# Find the actual position within the small field
# The position within the 3x3 field
pos_in_field_x = x % 3
pos_in_field_y = y % 3

# Determine which small field we need to restrict to
# The current player must place in the same small field that contains the last move
restricted_field_x = field_x
restricted_field_y = field_y

# Create a new grid with "!" for valid moves
result = []
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        # Determine which small field this cell belongs to
        field_i = i // 3
        field_j = j // 3
        
        # Check if this cell is in the restricted field
        if field_i == restricted_field_x and field_j == restricted_field_y:
            # This cell is in the restricted field
            # Check if it's empty (is ".")
            if grid[i][j] == '.':
                row.append('!')
            else:
                row.append(grid[i][j])
        else:
            # This cell is not in the restricted field
            # Check if it's empty
            if grid[i][j] == '.':
                row.append('!')
            else:
                row.append(grid[i][j])
    
    # Join the row with spaces
    result.append(' '.join(row))

# Output the result
for row in result:
    print(row)
```

```python

lines = []

    lines.append(line)

        # Check if this cell is in the restricted field
        if field_i == field_x and field_j == field_y:
            # This cell is in the restricted field
            if lines[i][j] == '.':
                row.append('!')
            else:
                row.append(lines[i][j])
        else:
            # This cell is not in the restricted field
            if lines[i][j] == '.':
                row.append('!')
            else:
                row.append(lines[i][j])
    
    # Join the row with spaces
    result.append(' '.join(row))

# Output the result
for row in result:
    print(row)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Two bears are playing tic-tac-toe via mail. It's boring for them to play usual tic-tac-toe game, so they are a playing modified version of this game. Here are its rules.

The game is played on the following field.

<image>

Players are making moves by turns. At first move a player can put his chip in any cell of any small field. For following moves, there are some restrictions: if during last move the opposite player put his chip to cell with coordinates (xl, yl) in some small field, the next move should be done in one of the cells of the small field with coordinates (xl, yl). For example, if in the first move a player puts his chip to lower left cell of central field, then the second player on his next move should put his chip into some cell of lower left field (pay attention to the first test case). If there are no free cells in the required field, the player can put his chip to any empty cell on any field.

You are given current state of the game and coordinates of cell in which the last move was done. You should find all cells in which the current player can put his chip.

A hare works as a postman in the forest, he likes to foul bears. Sometimes he changes the game field a bit, so the current state of the game could be unreachable. However, after his changes the cell where the last move was done is not empty. You don't need to find if the state is unreachable or not, just output possible next moves according to the rules.

Input

First 11 lines contains descriptions of table with 9 rows and 9 columns which are divided into 9 small fields by spaces and empty lines. Each small field is described by 9 characters without spaces and empty lines. character "x" (ASCII-code 120) means that the cell is occupied with chip of the first player, character "o" (ASCII-code 111) denotes a field occupied with chip of the second player, character "." (ASCII-code 46) describes empty cell.

The line after the table contains two integers x and y (1 ≤ x, y ≤ 9). They describe coordinates of the cell in table where the last move was done. Rows in the table are numbered from up to down and columns are numbered from left to right.

It's guaranteed that cell where the last move was done is filled with "x" or "o". Also, it's guaranteed that there is at least one empty cell. It's not guaranteed that current state of game is reachable.

Output

Output the field in same format with characters "!" (ASCII-code 33) on positions where the current player can put his chip. All other cells should not be modified.

Examples

Input

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... x.. ...

... ... ...
... ... ...
... ... ...
6 4


Output

... ... ... 
... ... ... 
... ... ... 

... ... ... 
... ... ... 
... x.. ... 

!!! ... ... 
!!! ... ... 
!!! ... ... 



Input

xoo x.. x..
ooo ... ...
ooo ... ...

x.. x.. x..
... ... ...
... ... ...

x.. x.. x..
... ... ...
... ... ...
7 4


Output

ooo !!! !!! 
ooo !!! !!!

!!! !!! !!! 
!!! !!! !!!

... ... ...
... ... ...

... ooo ...

... ... ...

!!! xox !!! 
!!! ooo !!!

!!! !!! !!! 

Note

In the first test case the first player made a move to lower left cell of central field, so the second player can put a chip only to cells of lower left field.

In the second test case the last move was done to upper left cell of lower central field, however all cells in upper left field are occupied, so the second player can put his chip to any empty cell.

In the third test case the last move was done to central cell of central field, so current player can put his chip to any cell of central field, which is already occupied, so he can move anywhere. Pay attention that this state of the game is unreachable.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a 9x9 tic-tac-toe board divided into 9 smaller 3x3 fields
2. Players take turns placing chips
3. The first move can be anywhere
4. After that, if the last move was in cell (x, y) of some small field, the next move must be in the small field that contains (x, y)
5. If that small field has no empty cells, the player can choose any empty cell
6. We need to output the board with "!" marking all possible next moves

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Parse the input
- Read 11 lines of the 9x9 grid (with spaces and empty lines)
- Parse the 9 small 3x3 fields
- Read the last move coordinates (x, y)

### Step 2: Determine the small field of the last move
- The coordinates (x, y) are 1-indexed
- Convert to 0-indexed: (x-1, y-1)
- Find which small field this cell belongs to
- Small field indices: (i // 3, j // 3) where i,j are 0-indexed row/col

### Step 3: Determine valid moves
- If the small field containing the last move has empty cells, the current player must move in that specific small field
- If the small field has no empty cells, the current player can move anywhere in the board
- Mark all valid positions with "!"

### Step 4: Output the result
- Replace empty cells (".") with "!" at valid positions
- Keep original values for other cells

## Implementation

```python
# Read the 11 lines of input
lines = []
for _ in range(11):
    line = input().strip()
    lines.append(line)

# Parse the 9x9 board into 9 small 3x3 fields
fields = []
for i in range(3):
    row = []
    for j in range(3):
        field_data = []
        for row_idx in range(3):
            line_data = lines[i * 4 + row_idx]
            field_data.append(line_data[3*j:3*j+3])
        row.append(field_data)
    fields.append(row)

# Read the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-indexed
x0, y0 = x - 1, y - 1

# Find which small field contains (x0, y0)
field_x = x0 // 3
field_y = y0 // 3

# Determine valid moves
possible_moves = []

# Check if the target small field has any empty cells
has_empty_in_target_field = False
target_field_rows = fields[field_x]
target_field_cols = field_y

for i in range(3):
    for j in range(3):
        if target_field_rows[i][j] == '.':
            has_empty_in_target_field = True
            break
    if has_empty_in_target_field:
        break

# If the target field has empty cells, current player can only move in that field
# If the target field has no empty cells, current player can move anywhere
if has_empty_in_target_field:
    # Only valid moves are in the target small field
    for i in range(3):
        for j in range(3):
            row_idx = field_x * 3 + i
            col_idx = field_y * 3 + j
            if fields[field_x][i][j] == '.':
                # Mark this position with "!"
                possible_moves.append((row_idx, col_idx, '!'))
else:
    # Can move anywhere in the board (except the last move position)
    for i in range(9):
        for j in range(9):
            if lines[i] and j < len(lines[i]) and lines[i][j] == '.':
                # Mark this position with "!"
                if i < 9 and j < 9:
                    possible_moves.append((i, j, '!'))

# Build output
output_lines = []
for i in range(9):
    row = []
    for j in range(9):
        # Find the original character
        if i < 9 and j < 9:
            original_char = lines[i][j]
            # Check if this position is a valid move
            if (i // 3, j // 3) == (field_x, field_y) and lines[i][j] == '.':
                row.append('!')
            elif lines[i][j] == '.' and (i // 3, j // 3) != (field_x, field_y):
                row.append('!')
            elif lines[i][j] == '.' and (i // 3, j // 3) == (field_x, field_y):
                row.append('!')
            else:
                row.append(original_char)
        else:
            row.append(original_char)
    output_lines.append(' '.join(row))

# Print output
for line in output_lines:
    print(line)
```

```python
for _ in range(9):
    line = input().strip()
    board.append(line)

# Read the last move coordinates
x, y = map(int, input().split())

# Convert to 0-indexed
row, col = x - 1, y - 1

# Determine which small field the last move was in
field_row = row // 3
field_col = col // 3

# Check if the required field has any free cells
has_free_cell_in_required_field = False
for r in range(3):
    for c in range(3):
        # Position in the required field
        board_row = field_row * 3 + r
        board_col = field_col * 3 + c
        if board[board_row][board_col] == '.':
            has_free_cell_in_required_field = True
            break
    if has_free_cell_in_required_field:
        break

# Create result board
result = []
for i in range(9):
    row_line = []
    for j in range(9):
        # Determine which small field this position is in
        field_i = i // 3
        field_j = j // 3
        
        # If we are in the required field and the cell is empty
        if field_i == field_row and field_j == field_col and board[i][j] == '.':
            row_line.append('!')
        # If we are not in the required field, then:
        # - if the required field has free cells, we can't move here
        # - if the required field has no free cells, we can move anywhere
        elif field_i != field_row or field_j != field_col:
            if not has_free_cell_in_required_field:
                # No free cells in required field, can move anywhere
                if board[i][j] == '.':
                    row_line.append('!')
            else:
                # Required field has free cells, can only move in required field
                row_line.append(board[i][j])
        else:
            # This case shouldn't happen because of the above conditions
            row_line.append(board[i][j])
    
    result.append(' '.join(row_line))

# Output the result
for row in result:
    print(row)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Yet another round on DecoForces is coming! Grandpa Maks wanted to participate in it but someone has stolen his precious sofa! And how can one perform well with such a major loss?

Fortunately, the thief had left a note for Grandpa Maks. This note got Maks to the sofa storehouse. Still he had no idea which sofa belongs to him as they all looked the same!

The storehouse is represented as matrix n × m. Every sofa takes two neighbouring by some side cells. No cell is covered by more than one sofa. There can be empty cells.

Sofa A is standing to the left of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that x_{a} < x_{b}, a is covered by A and b is covered by B. Sofa A is standing to the top of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that y_{a} < y_{b}, a is covered by A and b is covered by B. Right and bottom conditions are declared the same way. 

Note that in all conditions A ≠ B. Also some sofa A can be both to the top of another sofa B and to the bottom of it. The same is for left and right conditions.

The note also stated that there are cnt_{l} sofas to the left of Grandpa Maks's sofa, cnt_{r} — to the right, cnt_{t} — to the top and cnt_{b} — to the bottom.

Grandpa Maks asks you to help him to identify his sofa. It is guaranteed that there is no more than one sofa of given conditions.

Output the number of Grandpa Maks's sofa. If there is no such sofa that all the conditions are met for it then output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer number d (1 ≤ d ≤ 10^5) — the number of sofas in the storehouse.

The second line contains two integer numbers n, m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^5) — the size of the storehouse.

Next d lines contains four integer numbers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 (1 ≤ x_1, x_2 ≤ n, 1 ≤ y_1, y_2 ≤ m) — coordinates of the i-th sofa. It is guaranteed that cells (x_1, y_1) and (x_2, y_2) have common side, (x_1, y_1)  ≠  (x_2, y_2) and no cell is covered by more than one sofa.

The last line contains four integer numbers cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} (0 ≤ cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} ≤ d - 1).


-----Output-----

Print the number of the sofa for which all the conditions are met. Sofas are numbered 1 through d as given in input. If there is no such sofa then print -1.


-----Examples-----
Input
2
3 2
3 1 3 2
1 2 2 2
1 0 0 1

Output
1

Input
3
10 10
1 2 1 1
5 5 6 5
6 4 5 4
2 1 2 0

Output
2

Input
2
2 2
2 1 1 1
1 2 2 2
1 0 0 0

Output
-1



-----Note-----

Let's consider the second example.   The first sofa has 0 to its left, 2 sofas to its right ((1, 1) is to the left of both (5, 5) and (5, 4)), 0 to its top and 2 to its bottom (both 2nd and 3rd sofas are below).  The second sofa has cnt_{l} = 2, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 2 and cnt_{b} = 0.  The third sofa has cnt_{l} = 2, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 1 and cnt_{b} = 1. 

So the second one corresponds to the given conditions.

In the third example   The first sofa has cnt_{l} = 1, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 0 and cnt_{b} = 1.  The second sofa has cnt_{l} = 1, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 1 and cnt_{b} = 0. 

And there is no sofa with the set (1, 0, 0, 0) so the answer is -1.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a storehouse of size n×m with d sofas, each occupying two neighboring cells
2. For each sofa, we need to count how many other sofas are:
   - to its left: exists a cell in sofa A with x-coordinate < x-coordinate of a cell in sofa B
   - to its right: exists a cell in sofa A with x-coordinate > x-coordinate of a cell in sofa B
   - to its top: exists a cell in sofa A with y-coordinate < y-coordinate of a cell in sofa B
   - to its bottom: exists a cell in sofa A with y-coordinate > y-coordinate of a cell in sofa B
3. We're given target counts (cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b) and need to find which sofa matches these counts
4. If no sofa matches, return -1

## Key Insights

1. For a sofa to have another sofa to its left, there must be a cell in the first sofa with x < some cell in the second sofa
2. For a sofa to be to the left of another, the minimum x-coordinate of the first sofa must be less than the maximum x-coordinate of the second sofa
3. Similarly for other directions

## Solution Approach

For each sofa i, I need to:
1. Find the min and max x-coordinates of the sofa
2. Find the min and max y-coordinates of the sofa
3. For each other sofa j, check if it's to the left, right, top, or bottom of sofa i
4. Count how many sofas are in each position relative to sofa i

## Step-by-step Implementation

```python
def solve():
    d = int(input())
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    sofas = []
    for _ in range(d):
        x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
        sofas.append((x1, y1, x2, y2))
    
    cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # For each sofa, calculate its left, right, top, bottom counts
    for i in range(d):
        x1_i, y1_i, x2_i, y2_i = sofas[i]
        
        # Get min and max coordinates for this sofa
        min_x_i = min(x1_i, x2_i)
        max_x_i = max(x1_i, x2_i)
        min_y_i = min(y1_i, y2_i)
        max_y_i = max(y1_i, y2_i)
        
        # Count how many other sofas are to left, right, top, bottom of sofa i
        left_count = 0
        right_count = 0
        top_count = 0
        bottom_count = 0
        
        for j in range(d):
            if i == j:
                continue
                
            x1_j, y1_j, x2_j, y2_j = sofas[j]
            min_x_j = min(x1_j, x2_j)
            max_x_j = max(x1_j, x2_j)
            min_y_j = min(y1_j, y2_j)
            max_y_j = max(y1_j, y2_j)
            
            # Check if sofa j is to the left of sofa i
            # There exists a point in j with x < some point in i
            if min_x_j < max_x_i:
                left_count += 1
            
            # Check if sofa j is to the right of sofa i
            # There exists a point in j with x > some point in i
            if max_x_j > min_x_i:
                right_count += 1
            
            # Check if sofa j is to the top of sofa i
            # There exists a point in j with y < some point in i
            if min_y_j < max_y_i:
                top_count += 1
            
            # Check if sofa j is to the bottom of sofa i
            # There exists a point in j with y > some point in i
            if max_y_j > min_y_i:
                bottom_count += 1
        
        # Check if this sofa matches the required counts
        if left_count == cnt_l and right_count == cnt_r and top_count == cnt_t and bottom_count == cnt_b:
            return i + 1  # Sofa numbers are 1-indexed
    
    return -1

print(solve())
```

## Verification with Examples

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1:**
- Sofa 1: (3,1) to (3,2) → x=[3], y=[1,2]
- Sofa 2: (1,2) to (2,2) → x=[1,2], y=[2]
- Target: (1,0,0,1)

For sofa 1:
- Left: is there any sofa with min_x < max_x_i=3? Sofa 2 has min_x=1 → yes → left_count=1 ✓
- Right: is there any sofa with max_x > min_x_i=3? Sofa 1 has max_x=3, sofa 2 has max_x=2 → no → right_count=0 ✓  
- Top: is there any sofa with min_y < max_y_i=2? Sofa 2 has min_y=2 → no → top_count=0 ✓
- Bottom: is there any sofa with max_y > min_y_i=1? Sofa 2 has max_y=2 → yes → bottom_count=1 ✓

Matches target → answer is 1 ✓

**Example 2:**
- The solution checks each sofa against the target counts and finds sofa 2 matches → answer is 2 ✓

The logic looks correct. Here's the final code:

```python
d = int(input())
n, m = map(int, input().split())

sofas = []
for _ in range(d):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
    sofas.append((x1, y1, x2, y2))

cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b = map(int, input().split())

# For each sofa, calculate its left, right, top, bottom counts
for i in range(d):
    x1_i, y1_i, x2_i, y2_i = sofas[i]
    
    # Get min and max coordinates for this sofa
    min_x_i = min(x1_i, x2_i)
    max_x_i = max(x1_i, x2_i)
    min_y_i = min(y1_i, y2_i)
    max_y_i = max(y1_i, y2_i)
    
    # Count how many other sofas are to left, right, top, bottom of sofa i
    left_count = 0
    right_count = 0
    top_count = 0
    bottom_count = 0
    
    for j in range(d):
        if i == j:
            continue
            
        x1_j, y1_j, x2_j, y2_j = sofas[j]
        min_x_j = min(x1_j, x2_j)
        max_x_j = max(x1_j, x2_j)
        min_y_j = min(y1_j, y2_j)
        max_y_j = max(y1_j, y2_j)
        
        # Check if sofa j is to the left of sofa i
        if min_x_j < max_x_i:
            left_count += 1
        
        # Check if sofa j is to the right of sofa i
        if max_x_j > min_x_i:
            right_count += 1
        
        # Check if sofa j is to the top of sofa i
        if min_y_j < max_y_i:
            top_count += 1
        
        # Check if sofa j is to the bottom of sofa i
        if max_y_j > min_y_i:
            bottom_count += 1
    
    # Check if this sofa matches the required counts
    if left_count == cnt_l and right_count == cnt_r and top_count == cnt_t and bottom_count == cnt_b:
        print(i + 1)
        exit()

print(-1)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Yet another round on DecoForces is coming! Grandpa Maks wanted to participate in it but someone has stolen his precious sofa! And how can one perform well with such a major loss?

Fortunately, the thief had left a note for Grandpa Maks. This note got Maks to the sofa storehouse. Still he had no idea which sofa belongs to him as they all looked the same!

The storehouse is represented as matrix n × m. Every sofa takes two neighbouring by some side cells. No cell is covered by more than one sofa. There can be empty cells.

Sofa A is standing to the left of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that x_{a} < x_{b}, a is covered by A and b is covered by B. Sofa A is standing to the top of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that y_{a} < y_{b}, a is covered by A and b is covered by B. Right and bottom conditions are declared the same way. 

Note that in all conditions A ≠ B. Also some sofa A can be both to the top of another sofa B and to the bottom of it. The same is for left and right conditions.

The note also stated that there are cnt_{l} sofas to the left of Grandpa Maks's sofa, cnt_{r} — to the right, cnt_{t} — to the top and cnt_{b} — to the bottom.

Grandpa Maks asks you to help him to identify his sofa. It is guaranteed that there is no more than one sofa of given conditions.

Output the number of Grandpa Maks's sofa. If there is no such sofa that all the conditions are met for it then output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer number d (1 ≤ d ≤ 10^5) — the number of sofas in the storehouse.

The second line contains two integer numbers n, m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^5) — the size of the storehouse.

Next d lines contains four integer numbers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 (1 ≤ x_1, x_2 ≤ n, 1 ≤ y_1, y_2 ≤ m) — coordinates of the i-th sofa. It is guaranteed that cells (x_1, y_1) and (x_2, y_2) have common side, (x_1, y_1)  ≠  (x_2, y_2) and no cell is covered by more than one sofa.

The last line contains four integer numbers cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} (0 ≤ cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} ≤ d - 1).


-----Output-----

Print the number of the sofa for which all the conditions are met. Sofas are numbered 1 through d as given in input. If there is no such sofa then print -1.


-----Examples-----
Input
2
3 2
3 1 3 2
1 2 2 2
1 0 0 1

Output
1

Input
3
10 10
1 2 1 1
5 5 6 5
6 4 5 4
2 1 2 0

Output
2

Input
2
2 2
2 1 1 1
1 2 2 2
1 0 0 0

Output
-1



-----Note-----

Let's consider the second example.   The first sofa has 0 to its left, 2 sofas to its right ((1, 1) is to the left of both (5, 5) and (5, 4)), 0 to its top and 2 to its bottom (both 2nd and 3rd sofas are below).  The second sofa has cnt_{l} = 2, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 2 and cnt_{b} = 0.  The third sofa has cnt_{l} = 2, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 1 and cnt_{b} = 1. 

So the second one corresponds to the given conditions.

In the third example   The first sofa has cnt_{l} = 1, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 0 and cnt_{b} = 1.  The second sofa has cnt_{l} = 1, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 1 and cnt_{b} = 0. 

And there is no sofa with the set (1, 0, 0, 0) so the answer is -1.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). ### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a storehouse represented as an n×m matrix
2. Each sofa occupies two adjacent cells (sharing a side)
3. For each sofa, we need to count how many other sofas are:
   - To its left: exists a cell in the left sofa with smaller x-coordinate than a cell in the current sofa
   - To its right: exists a cell in the right sofa with larger x-coordinate than a cell in the current sofa
   - To its top: exists a cell in the top sofa with smaller y-coordinate than a cell in the current sofa
   - To its bottom: exists a cell in the bottom sofa with larger y-coordinate than a cell in the current sofa

4. We're given the expected counts (cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b) and need to find which sofa has exactly those counts.

## Key Insights

1. For a sofa to have another sofa to its left: there must exist a cell with smaller x-coordinate
2. For a sofa to have another sofa to its right: there must exist a cell with larger x-coordinate
3. For a sofa to have another sofa to its top: there must exist a cell with smaller y-coordinate
4. For a sofa to have another sofa to its bottom: there must exist a cell with larger y-coordinate

## Algorithm

1. For each sofa, calculate how many other sofas are to its left, right, top, and bottom
2. Compare these counts with the given requirements
3. Return the sofa number (1-indexed) that matches exactly, or -1 if none

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Parse input
2. For each sofa, determine its min/max x and y coordinates
3. For each sofa, count how many other sofas have:
   - Left: a cell with smaller x than current sofa's min x
   - Right: a cell with larger x than current sofa's max x
   - Top: a cell with smaller y than current sofa's min y
   - Bottom: a cell with larger y than current sofa's max y
4. Compare with given counts and find matching sofa

```python
def solve():
    d = int(input())
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    sofas = []
    for i in range(d):
        x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
        sofas.append((x1, y1, x2, y2))
    
    cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # For each sofa, calculate its bounding box
    # min_x = min(x1, x2), max_x = max(x1, x2)
    # min_y = min(y1, y2), max_y = max(y1, y2)
    
    for i in range(d):
        x1, y1, x2, y2 = sofas[i]
        min_x = min(x1, x2)
        max_x = max(x1, x2)
        min_y = min(y1, y2)
        max_y = max(y1, y2)
        
        # Count sofas to the left: other sofa has a cell with smaller x
        left_count = 0
        for j in range(d):
            if i == j:
                continue
            x1_j, y1_j, x2_j, y2_j = sofas[j]
            min_x_j = min(x1_j, x2_j)
            max_x_j = max(x1_j, x2_j)
            min_y_j = min(y1_j, y2_j)
            max_y_j = max(y1_j, y2_j)
            
            # Sofa j is to the left of sofa i if any of its cells has x < min_x of sofa i
            if min_x_j < min_x:
                left_count += 1
        
        # Count sofas to the right: other sofa has a cell with larger x
        right_count = 0
        for j in range(d):
            if i == j:
                continue
            x1_j, y1_j, x2_j, y2_j = sofas[j]
            min_x_j = min(x1_j, x2_j)
            max_x_j = max(x1_j, x2_j)
            min_y_j = min(y1_j, y2_j)
            max_y_j = max(y1_j, y2_j)
            
            # Sofa j is to the right of sofa i if any of its cells has x > max_x of sofa i
            if max_x_j > max_x:
                right_count += 1
        
        # Count sofas to the top: other sofa has a cell with smaller y
        top_count = 0
        for j in range(d):
            if i == j:
                continue
            x1_j, y1_j, x2_j, y2_j = sofas[j]
            min_x_j = min(x1_j, x2_j)
            max_x_j = max(x1_j, x2_j)
            min_y_j = min(y1_j, y2_j)
            max_y_j = max(y1_j, y2_j)
            
            # Sofa j is to the top of sofa i if any of its cells has y < min_y of sofa i
            if min_y_j < min_y:
                top_count += 1
        
        # Count sofas to the bottom: other sofa has a cell with larger y
        bottom_count = 0
        for j in range(d):
            if i == j:
                continue
            x1_j, y1_j, x2_j, y2_j = sofas[j]
            min_x_j = min(x1_j, x2_j)
            max_x_j = max(x1_j, x2_j)
            min_y_j = min(y1_j, y2_j)
            max_y_j = max(y1_j, y2_j)
            
            # Sofa j is to the bottom of sofa i if any of its cells has y > max_y of sofa i
            if max_y_j > max_y:
                bottom_count += 1
        
        # Check if this sofa matches the required counts
        if left_count == cnt_l and right_count == cnt_r and top_count == cnt_t and bottom_count == cnt_b:
            print(i + 1)
            return
    
    print(-1)

solve()
```

The note says: "Sofa A is standing to the left of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that x_a < x_b, a is covered by A and b is covered by B."

This means: A is to the left of B if there's a cell in A with x-coordinate < a cell in B.

So for sofa i to have a sofa j to its left: there must exist a cell in j with x < min_x of sofa i.

For sofa i to have a sofa j to its right: there must exist a cell in j with x > max_x of sofa i.

For sofa i to have a sofa j to its top: there must exist a cell in j with y < min_y of sofa i.

For sofa i to have a sofa j to its bottom: there must exist a cell in j with y > max_y of sofa i.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a storehouse of size n×m with d sofas
2. Each sofa occupies 2 adjacent cells (sharing a side)
3. We need to find which sofa has exactly `cnt_l` sofas to its left, `cnt_r` to its right, `cnt_t` to its top, and `cnt_b` to its bottom
4. "To the left" means there exists a cell in sofa A with x-coordinate < x-coordinate of a cell in sofa B
5. Similarly for top, bottom, and right

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Understand the relationship
For any two sofas A and B, A is to the left of B if there exists a cell in A with smaller x-coordinate than any cell in B. Actually, if A has any cell with x < x of some cell in B.

### Step 2: Algorithm approach
For each sofa, I need to:
1. Check how many other sofas are to its left (have at least one cell with smaller x-coordinate)
2. Check how many other sofas are to its right (have at least one cell with larger x-coordinate)
3. Check how many other sofas are to its top (have at least one cell with smaller y-coordinate)
4. Check how many other sofas are to its bottom (have at least one cell with larger y-coordinate)

### Step 3: Implementation details
For each sofa, I'll:
- Extract its x-coordinates and y-coordinates
- For each other sofa, check if it's to the left/right/top/bottom of the current sofa

### Step 4: Optimization
Instead of checking every pair (O(d²)), we can precompute:
- Minimum x-coordinate of each sofa
- Maximum x-coordinate of each sofa
- Minimum y-coordinate of each sofa  
- Maximum y-coordinate of each sofa

Similarly, A is to the right of B if max_x(A) > min_x(B)

- A is to the left of B if there exists x1 in A and x2 in B such that x1 < x2

- A is to the right of B if max_x(A) > min_x(B)
- A is to the top of B if min_y(A) < max_y(B)
- A is to the bottom of B if max_y(A) > min_y(B)

1. Read input
2. For each sofa, compute its min_x, max_x, min_y, max_y
3. For each sofa i, count how many sofas j (j ≠ i) satisfy:
   - j is to the left of i: min_x(j) < max_x(i)
   - j is to the right of i: max_x(j) > min_x(i)
   - j is to the top of i: min_y(j) < max_y(i)
   - j is to the bottom of i: max_y(j) > min_y(i)
4. Check if these counts match the given cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b
5. Return the sofa number (1-indexed) if found, otherwise -1

If sofa A is to the left of sofa B, it means A has a cell with smaller x than a cell in B.

- Soas to the left of sofa B: A such that min_x(A) < max_x(B)
- Sofas to the right of sofa B: A such that max_x(A) > min_x(B)
- Sofas to the top of sofa B: A such that min_y(A) < max_y(B)
- Sofas to the bottom of sofa B: A such that max_y(A) > min_y(B)

- Left of it: sofas where min_x < max_x(1) = 1 → min_x < 1 → no sofas (all min_x >= 1)
- Right of it: sofas where max_x > min_x(1) = 1 → max_x > 1 → all sofas (sofa 2,3) → count = 2
- Top of it: sofas where min_y < max_y(1) = 2 → min_y < 2 → sofa 3 has min_y=4 >= 2, sofa 2 has min_y=5 >= 2 → count = 0
- Bottom of it: sofas where max_y > min_y(1) = 1 → max_y > 1 → all sofas (sofa 2: max_y=5>1, sofa 3: max_y=4>1) → count = 2

Wait, no: if A is to the left of B, then A has a cell with smaller x than B.
So for sofa B, the count of sofas to its left is the count of sofas A where min_x(A) < max_x(B).

This is correct.

```python
d = int(input())
n, m = map(int, input().split())

sofas = []
for i in range(d):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
    min_x = min(x1, x2)
    max_x = max(x1, x2)
    min_y = min(y1, y2)
    max_y = max(y1, y2)
    sofas.append((min_x, max_x, min_y, max_y))

cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b = map(int, input().split())

result = -1

for i in range(d):
    min_x_i, max_x_i, min_y_i, max_y_i = sofas[i]
    
    left_count = 0
    right_count = 0
    top_count = 0
    bottom_count = 0
    
    for j in range(d):
        if i == j:
            continue
            
        min_x_j, max_x_j, min_y_j, max_y_j = sofas[j]
        
        # j is to the left of i if min_x(j) < max_x(i)
        if min_x_j < max_x_i:
            left_count += 1
            
        # j is to the right of i if max_x(j) > min_x(i)
        if max_x_j > min_x_i:
            right_count += 1
            
        # j is to the top of i if min_y(j) < max_y(i)
        if min_y_j < max_y_i:
            top_count += 1
            
        # j is to the bottom of i if max_y(j) > min_y(i)
        if max_y_j > min_y_i:
            bottom_count += 1
    
    if left_count == cnt_l and right_count == cnt_r and top_count == cnt_t and bottom_count == cnt_b:
        result = i + 1
        break

print(result)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Yet another round on DecoForces is coming! Grandpa Maks wanted to participate in it but someone has stolen his precious sofa! And how can one perform well with such a major loss?

Fortunately, the thief had left a note for Grandpa Maks. This note got Maks to the sofa storehouse. Still he had no idea which sofa belongs to him as they all looked the same!

The storehouse is represented as matrix n × m. Every sofa takes two neighbouring by some side cells. No cell is covered by more than one sofa. There can be empty cells.

Sofa A is standing to the left of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that x_{a} < x_{b}, a is covered by A and b is covered by B. Sofa A is standing to the top of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that y_{a} < y_{b}, a is covered by A and b is covered by B. Right and bottom conditions are declared the same way. 

Note that in all conditions A ≠ B. Also some sofa A can be both to the top of another sofa B and to the bottom of it. The same is for left and right conditions.

The note also stated that there are cnt_{l} sofas to the left of Grandpa Maks's sofa, cnt_{r} — to the right, cnt_{t} — to the top and cnt_{b} — to the bottom.

Grandpa Maks asks you to help him to identify his sofa. It is guaranteed that there is no more than one sofa of given conditions.

Output the number of Grandpa Maks's sofa. If there is no such sofa that all the conditions are met for it then output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer number d (1 ≤ d ≤ 10^5) — the number of sofas in the storehouse.

The second line contains two integer numbers n, m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^5) — the size of the storehouse.

Next d lines contains four integer numbers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 (1 ≤ x_1, x_2 ≤ n, 1 ≤ y_1, y_2 ≤ m) — coordinates of the i-th sofa. It is guaranteed that cells (x_1, y_1) and (x_2, y_2) have common side, (x_1, y_1)  ≠  (x_2, y_2) and no cell is covered by more than one sofa.

The last line contains four integer numbers cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} (0 ≤ cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} ≤ d - 1).


-----Output-----

Print the number of the sofa for which all the conditions are met. Sofas are numbered 1 through d as given in input. If there is no such sofa then print -1.


-----Examples-----
Input
2
3 2
3 1 3 2
1 2 2 2
1 0 0 1

Output
1

Input
3
10 10
1 2 1 1
5 5 6 5
6 4 5 4
2 1 2 0

Output
2

Input
2
2 2
2 1 1 1
1 2 2 2
1 0 0 0

Output
-1
