### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a storehouse represented as an n×m grid
2. Each sofa occupies two adjacent cells (sharing a side)
3. For any sofa, we need to count how many other sofas are:
   - To the left of it (some cell of the sofa has x < some cell of another sofa)
   - To the right of it (some cell of the sofa has x > some cell of another sofa)
   - To the top of it (some cell of the sofa has y < some cell of another sofa)
   - To the bottom of it (some cell of the sofa has y > some cell of another sofa)

4. We're given the expected counts (cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b) and need to find which sofa matches these conditions.

## Key Insights

1. For a sofa to have "cnt_l" sofas to its left, there must be cnt_l sofas that have at least one cell with x-coordinate less than any cell of our sofa.

2. For a sofa to have "cnt_r" sofas to its right, there must be cnt_r sofas that have at least one cell with x-coordinate greater than any cell of our sofa.

3. Similarly for top/bottom conditions with y-coordinates.

4. For each sofa, we need to:
   - Find the minimum x-coordinate among its two cells
   - Find the maximum x-coordinate among its two cells
   - Find the minimum y-coordinate among its two cells
   - Find the maximum y-coordinate among its two cells

5. Then for each other sofa, check if:
   - Any cell of that sofa has x < min_x of current sofa → counts as "to the left"
   - Any cell of that sofa has x > max_x of current sofa → counts as "to the right"
   - Any cell of that sofa has y < min_y of current sofa → counts as "to the top"
   - Any cell of that sofa has y > max_y of current sofa → counts as "to the bottom"

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. Read input data
2. For each sofa, calculate its bounding box (min/max x, min/max y)
3. For each sofa, count how many other sofas are to its left, right, top, bottom
4. Compare with given counts and return the matching sofa number
5. If no sofa matches, return -1

```python
def solve():
    d = int(input())
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    sofas = []
    for i in range(d):
        x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
        sofas.append((x1, y1, x2, y2))
    
    cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # For each sofa, calculate its bounding box
    def get_bounds(sofa):
        x1, y1, x2, y2 = sofa
        min_x = min(x1, x2)
        max_x = max(x1, x2)
        min_y = min(y1, y2)
        max_y = max(y1, y2)
        return min_x, max_x, min_y, max_y
    
    # Check if a sofa has the given count conditions
    def satisfies_conditions(sofa_idx, cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b):
        min_x, max_x, min_y, max_y = get_bounds( sofas[sofa_idx] )
        
        left_count = 0
        right_count = 0
        top_count = 0
        bottom_count = 0
        
        for i in range(d):
            if i == sofa_idx:
                continue
                
            x1, y1, x2, y2 = sofas[i]
            min_x2 = min(x1, x2)
            max_x2 = max(x1, x2)
            min_y2 = min(y1, y2)
            max_y2 = max(y1, y2)
            
            # Check if sofa i is to the left of sofa sofa_idx
            if min_x2 < min_x:  # any cell of sofa i has x < any cell of sofa sofa_idx
                left_count += 1
                
            # Check if sofa i is to the right of sofa sofa_idx
            if max_x2 > max_x:  # any cell of sofa i has x > any cell of sofa sofa_idx
                right_count += 1
                
            # Check if sofa i is to the top of sofa sofa_idx
            if min_y2 < min_y:  # any cell of sofa i has y < any cell of sofa sofa_idx
                top_count += 1
                
            # Check if sofa i is to the bottom of sofa sofa_idx
            if max_y2 > max_y:  # any cell of sofa i has y > any cell of sofa sofa_idx
                bottom_count += 1
        
        return (left_count == cnt_l and 
                right_count == cnt_r and 
                top_count == cnt_t and 
                bottom_count == cnt_b)
    
    # Find the sofa that satisfies the conditions
    for i in range(d):
        if satisfies_conditions(i, cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b):
            return i + 1  # Sofa numbers are 1-indexed
    
    return -1

print(solve())
```

The solution works as follows:

1. **Input parsing**: Read the number of sofas, grid dimensions, and sofa coordinates
2. **Bounding box calculation**: For each sofa, find the minimum and maximum x and y coordinates of its two cells
3. **Condition checking**: For each sofa, count how many other sofas are to its left, right, top, and bottom by checking if any cell of the other sofa has coordinates that satisfy the condition
4. **Result**: Return the 1-indexed sofa number that matches the given counts, or -1 if none exists

Time complexity: O(d²) where d is the number of sofas, which is acceptable given the constraints.

def solve():
    d = int(input())
    n, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    sofas = []
    for i in range(d):
        x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
        sofas.append((x1, y1, x2, y2))
    
    cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b = map(int, input().split())
    
    # For each sofa, calculate its bounding box
    def get_bounds(sofa):
        x1, y1, x2, y2 = sofa
        min_x = min(x1, x2)
        max_x = max(x1, x2)
        min_y = min(y1, y2)
        max_y = max(y1, y2)
        return min_x, max_x, min_y, max_y
    
    # Check if a sofa has the given count conditions
    def satisfies_conditions(sofa_idx, cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b):
        min_x, max_x, min_y, max_y = get_bounds( sofas[sofa_idx] )
        
        left_count = 0
        right_count = 0
        top_count = 0
        bottom_count = 0
        
        for i in range(d):
            if i == sofa_idx:
                continue
                
            x1, y1, x2, y2 = sofas[i]
            min_x2 = min(x1, x2)
            max_x2 = max(x1, x2)
            min_y2 = min(y1, y2)
            max_y2 = max(y1, y2)
            
            # Check if sofa i is to the left of sofa sofa_idx
            if min_x2 < min_x:  # any cell of sofa i has x < any cell of sofa sofa_idx
                left_count += 1
                
            # Check if sofa i is to the right of sofa sofa_idx
            if max_x2 > max_x:  # any cell of sofa i has x > any cell of sofa sofa_idx
                right_count += 1
                
            # Check if sofa i is to the top of sofa sofa_idx
            if min_y2 < min_y:  # any cell of sofa i has y < any cell of sofa sofa_idx
                top_count += 1
                
            # Check if sofa i is to the bottom of sofa sofa_idx
            if max_y2 > max_y:  # any cell of sofa i has y > any cell of sofa sofa_idx
                bottom_count += 1
        
        return (left_count == cnt_l and 
                right_count == cnt_r and 
                top_count == cnt_t and 
                bottom_count == cnt_b)
    
    # Find the sofa that satisfies the conditions
    for i in range(d):
        if satisfies_conditions(i, cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b):
            return i + 1  # Sofa numbers are 1-indexed
    
    return -1

print(solve())

The storehouse is represented as matrix n × m. Every sofa takes two neighbouring by some side cells. No cell is covered by more than one sofa. There can be empty cells.

Sofa A is standing to the left of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that x_{a} < x_{b}, a is covered by A and b is covered by B. Sofa A is standing to the top of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that y_{a} < y_{b}, a is covered by A and b is covered by B. Right and bottom conditions are declared the same way. 

Note that in all conditions A ≠ B. Also some sofa A can be both to the top of another sofa B and to the bottom of it. The same is for left and right conditions.

The note also stated that there are cnt_{l} sofas to the left of Grandpa Maks's sofa, cnt_{r} — to the right, cnt_{t} — to the top and cnt_{b} — to the bottom.

Grandpa Maks asks you to help him to identify his sofa. It is guaranteed that there is no more than one sofa of given conditions.

Output the number of Grandpa Maks's sofa. If there is no such sofa that all the conditions are met for it then output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer number d (1 ≤ d ≤ 10^5) — the number of sofas in the storehouse.

The second line contains two integer numbers n, m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^5) — the size of the storehouse.

Next d lines contains four integer numbers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 (1 ≤ x_1, x_2 ≤ n, 1 ≤ y_1, y_2 ≤ m) — coordinates of the i-th sofa. It is guaranteed that cells (x_1, y_1) and (x_2, y_2) have common side, (x_1, y_1)  ≠  (x_2, y_2) and no cell is covered by more than one sofa.

The last line contains four integer numbers cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} (0 ≤ cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} ≤ d - 1).


-----Output-----

Print the number of the sofa for which all the conditions are met. Sofas are numbered 1 through d as given in input. If there is no such sofa then print -1.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a storehouse represented as an n×m matrix
2. Each sofa occupies 2 adjacent cells (sharing a side)
3. We need to find which sofa has exactly `cnt_l` sofas to its left, `cnt_r` to its right, `cnt_t` to its top, and `cnt_b` to its bottom
4. A sofa A is to the left of sofa B if there's a cell in A with smaller x-coordinate than any cell in B
5. Similarly for top, right, and bottom conditions

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Understand the conditions
- Sofa A is to the left of sofa B if there exists a cell (x₁,y₁) in A and (x₂,y₂) in B such that x₁ < x₂
- Sofa A is to the top of sofa B if there exists a cell (x₁,y₁) in A and (x₂,y₂) in B such that y₁ < y₂
- The conditions are NOT about the entire sofa being on one side, but about existence of at least one cell pair

### Step 2: For each sofa, count how many other sofas are to its left, right, top, bottom

For a given sofa S with cells (x₁,y₁) and (x₂,y₂):
- To count sofas to its left: check if any cell in other sofa has x > min(x₁,x₂) and x < max(x₁,x₂) (no, actually we need to check if there's a cell in other sofa with x > some cell in S)
- Actually: Sofa T is to the left of sofa S if there's a cell in T with x < some cell in S

Wait, let me re-read: "Sofa A is standing to the left of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that x_a < x_b, a is covered by A and b is covered by B"

So for sofa A to be to the left of sofa B: there exists a cell in A with x < x of some cell in B.

For sofa B to be to the left of sofa A: there exists a cell in B with x < x of some cell in A.

### Step 3: Algorithm
1. For each sofa i, we need to check all other sofas j:
   - Check if sofa j is to the left of sofa i: is there any cell in j with x < min(x of cells in i)? 
     Actually: is there any cell in j with x < any x in i? Yes, if min(x_j) < max(x_i)
   - Similarly for other directions

2. For sofa i, count:
   - left_count: number of sofas j where min(x_j) < max(x_i) 
   - right_count: number of sofas j where max(x_j) > min(x_i)
   - top_count: number of sofas j where min(y_j) < max(y_i)
   - bottom_count: number of sofas j where max(y_j) > min(y_i)

Wait, this isn't quite right. Let me think again.

For sofa j to be to the left of sofa i: there exists a cell in j and a cell in i such that x_j < x_i.

This happens if min(x_j) < max(x_i). Because if min(x_j) < max(x_i), then there's some cell with x_j < max(x_i), and since there's a cell in i with x_i = max(x_i), we have x_j < x_i.

Similarly:
- j is to the left of i if min(x_j) < max(x_i)
- j is to the right of i if max(x_j) > min(x_i)
- j is to the top of i if min(y_j) < max(y_i)
- j is to the bottom of i if max(y_j) > min(y_i)

### Step 4: Implementation

```python
d = int(input())
n, m = map(int, input().split())

sofas = []
for _ in range(d):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
    sofas.append((x1, y1, x2, y2))

cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b = map(int, input().split())

def count_left_right_bottom(sofa, other_sofas):
    left_count = 0
    right_count = 0
    top_count = 0
    bottom_count = 0
    
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = sofa
    min_x, max_x = min(x1, x2), max(x1, x2)
    min_y, max_y = min(y1, y2), max(y1, y2)
    
    for other_sofa in other_sofas:
        ox1, oy1, ox2, oy2 = other_sofa
        o_min_x, o_max_x = min(ox1, ox2), max(ox1, ox2)
        o_min_y, o_max_y = min(oy1, oy2), max(oy1, oy2)
        
        # Check if other sofa is to the left of current sofa
        if o_min_x < max_x:
            left_count += 1
            
        # Check if other sofa is to the right of current sofa
        if o_max_x > min_x:
            right_count += 1
            
        # Check if other sofa is to the top of current sofa
        if o_min_y < max_y:
            top_count += 1
            
        # Check if other sofa is to the bottom of current sofa
        if o_max_y > min_y:
            bottom_count += 1
    
    return left_count, right_count, top_count, bottom_count

# For each sofa, calculate the counts
result = -1
for i in range(d):
    left, right, top, bottom = count_left_right_bottom(sofas[i], sofas[:i] + sofas[i+1:])
    
    if left == cnt_l and right == cnt_r and top == cnt_t and bottom == cnt_b:
        result = i + 1
        break

print(result)
```

```python
def get_counts(sofa):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = sofa
    min_x, max_x = min(x1, x2), max(x1, x2)
    min_y, max_y = min(y1, y2), max(y1, y2)

            right_count += 1

            top_count += 1
            
        # Other sofa is to the bottom of current sofa if max_y of other > min_y of current
        if o_max_y > min_y:
            bottom_count += 1
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a storehouse represented as an n×m matrix
2. Each sofa occupies two adjacent cells (sharing a side)
3. We need to find which sofa has exactly `cnt_l` sofas to its left, `cnt_r` to its right, `cnt_t` to its top, and `cnt_b` to its bottom
4. A sofa A is to the left of sofa B if there's a cell in A with smaller x-coordinate than any cell in B
5. Similarly for top, right, and bottom

## Key Insights

1. For each sofa, we need to count how many other sofas have cells with smaller x-coordinates (left condition)
2. For each sofa, we need to count how many other sofas have cells with larger x-coordinates (right condition)
3. Same for y-coordinates (top and bottom)

4. For a sofa to be to the left of another, at least one cell of the first sofa must have smaller x-coordinate than at least one cell of the second sofa.

5. We can simplify this: For two sofas A and B, A is to the left of B if min(x-coordinates of A) < max(x-coordinates of B) OR if min(x-coordinates of A) < max(x-coordinates of B) OR if min(x-coordinates of A) < max(x-coordinates of B).

So A is to the left of B if there exists at least one cell in A with x-coordinate strictly less than some cell in B.

This means: A is to the left of B if min_x_A < max_x_B.

Similarly:
- A is to the left of B if min_x_A < max_x_B
- A is to the right of B if max_x_A > min_x_B  
- A is to the top of B if min_y_A < max_y_B
- A is to the bottom of B if max_y_A > min_y_B

Actually, let me think again:
- A is to the left of B if there exists a cell in A with x < some cell in B. This means min_x_A < max_x_B
- A is to the right of B if there exists a cell in A with x > some cell in B. This means max_x_A > min_x_B
- A is to the top of B if there exists a cell in A with y < some cell in B. This means min_y_A < max_y_B
- A is to the bottom of B if there exists a cell in A with y > some cell in B. This means max_y_A > min_y_B

Wait, that's not quite right. For A to be to the left of B, we need: there exists a cell in A with x < some cell in B. This means min_x_A < max_x_B.

For A to be to the right of B: there exists a cell in A with x > some cell in B. This means max_x_A > min_x_B.

For A to be to the top of B: there exists a cell in A with y < some cell in B. This means min_y_A < max_y_B.

For A to be to the bottom of B: there exists a cell in A with y > some cell in B. This means max_y_A > min_y_B.

## Algorithm

1. For each sofa, calculate its min and max x and y coordinates
2. For each sofa i, count:
   - sofas to its left: number of sofas j where min_x_j < max_x_i
   - sofas to its right: number of sofas j where max_x_j > min_x_i  
   - sofas to its top: number of sofas j where min_y_j < max_y_i
   - sofas to its bottom: number of sofas j where max_y_j > min_y_i

Wait, I think I have this backward in my reasoning.

Let me reconsider: "Sofa A is standing to the left of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that x_a < x_b, a is covered by A and b is covered by B."

This means A is to the left of B if there's a cell in A with x-coordinate < some cell in B.

So if we have sofa A and sofa B, A is left of B if min_x_A < max_x_B (because if the minimum x of A is less than the maximum x of B, then there's at least one cell in A with x less than some cell in B).

Similarly:
- A is to the right of B if max_x_A > min_x_B
- A is to the top of B if min_y_A < max_y_B
- A is to the bottom of B if max_y_A > min_y_B

But the "to the left of" condition is for A being left of B. In our problem, we're counting how many sofas are to the left of a given sofa.

For a given sofa S, the number of sofas to its left = count of sofas T where T is to the left of S.

T is to the left of S if min_x_T < max_x_S.

So for sofa S, count of sofas to its left = count of sofas T where min_x_T < max_x_S.

Similarly:
- Count of sofas to its right = count of sofas T where max_x_T > min_x_S
- Count of sofas to its top = count of sofas T where min_y_T < max_y_S
- Count of sofas to its bottom = count of sofas T where max_y_T > min_y_S

## Implementation

```python
d = int(input())
n, m = map(int, input().split())

sofas = []
for i in range(d):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
    # Calculate min and max coordinates for this sofa
    min_x = min(x1, x2)
    max_x = max(x1, x2)
    min_y = min(y1, y2)
    max_y = max(y1, y2)
    sofas.append((min_x, max_x, min_y, max_y))

cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b = map(int, input().split())

# For each sofa, calculate the counts
result = -1
for i in range(d):
    min_x_i, max_x_i, min_y_i, max_y_i = sofas[i]
    
    # Count sofas to the left of sofa i
    left_count = 0
    for j in range(d):
        if j != i:
            min_x_j, max_x_j, min_y_j, max_y_j = sofas[j]
            if min_x_j < max_x_i:  # sofa j is to the left of sofa i
                left_count += 1
    
    # Count sofas to the right of sofa i
    right_count = 0
    for j in range(d):
        if j != i:
            min_x_j, max_x_j, min_y_j, max_y_j = sofas[j]
            if max_x_j > min_x_i:  # sofa j is to the right of sofa i
                right_count += 1
    
    # Count sofas to the top of sofa i
    top_count = 0
    for j in range(d):
        if j != i:
            min_x_j, max_x_j, min_y_j, max_y_j = sofas[j]
            if min_y_j < max_y_i:  # sofa j is to the top of sofa i
                top_count += 1
    
    # Count sofas to the bottom of sofa i
    bottom_count = 0
    for j in range(d):
        if j != i:
            min_x_j, max_x_j, min_y_j, max_y_j = sofas[j]
            if max_y_j > min_y_i:  # sofa j is to the bottom of sofa i
                bottom_count += 1
    
    # Check if this sofa matches the required conditions
    if left_count == cnt_l and right_count == cnt_r and top_count == cnt_t and bottom_count == cnt_b:
        result = i + 1  # 1-indexed
        break

print(result)
```

Wait, this solution has O(d²) complexity which might be too slow for d up to 10^5.

Let me optimize it. Instead of checking every pair, I can precompute sorted lists.

```python
d = int(input())
n, m = map(int, input().split())

sofas = []
for i in range(d):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
    min_x = min(x1, x2)
    max_x = max(x1, x2)
    min_y = min(y1, y2)
    max_y = max(y1, y2)
    sofas.append((min_x, max_x, min_y, max_y))

cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b = map(int, input().split())

# Precompute all min_x, max_x, min_y, max_y
min_x_list = [sofas[i][0] for i in range(d)]
max_x_list = [sofas[i][1] for i in range(d)]
min_y_list = [sofas[i][2] for i in range(d)]
max_y_list = [sofas[i][3] for i in range(d)]

# For a sofa i, count sofas to its left: count of sofas j where min_x_j < max_x_i
# This is equivalent to: count of sofas with min_x < max_x_i
# We can use binary search since the list is sorted

# Create sorted lists for efficient counting
sorted_min_x = sorted(min_x_list)
sorted_max_x = sorted(max_x_list)
sorted_min_y = sorted(min_y_list)
sorted_max_y = sorted(max_y_list)

result = -1
for i in range(d):
    min_x_i, max_x_i, min_y_i, max_y_i = sofas[i]
    
    # Count sofas to the left of sofa i (sofas j where min_x_j < max_x_i)
    # Use binary search to find position where max_x_i would be inserted
    left_count = 0
    # Count of sofas with min_x_j < max_x_i
    # This is the number of elements in sorted_min_x that are < max_x_i
    left_count = bisect.bisect_left(sorted_min_x, max_x_i)
    
    # Count sofas to the right of sofa i (sofas j where max_x_j > min_x_i)
    # This is the number of elements in sorted_max_x that are > min_x_i
    right_count = 0
    right_count = d - bisect.bisect_right(sorted_max_x, min_x_i)
    
    # Count sofas to the top of sofa i (sofas j where min_y_j < max_y_i)
    top_count = 0
    top_count = bisect.bisect_left(sorted_min_y, max_y_i)
    
    # Count sofas to the bottom of sofa i (sofas j where max_y_j > min_y_i)
    bottom_count = 0
    bottom_count = d - bisect.bisect_right(sorted_max_y, min_y_i)
    
    if left_count == cnt_l and right_count == cnt_r and top_count == cnt_t and bottom_count == cnt_b:
        result = i + 1
        break

print(result)
```

Wait, I need to be careful about the "to the left" definition. Let me double-check:

A sofa j is to the left of sofa i if min_x_j < max_x_i.

Yes, that's correct.

The code above is almost right, but I need to import bisect.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Yet another round on DecoForces is coming! Grandpa Maks wanted to participate in it but someone has stolen his precious sofa! And how can one perform well with such a major loss?

Fortunately, the thief had left a note for Grandpa Maks. This note got Maks to the sofa storehouse. Still he had no idea which sofa belongs to him as they all looked the same!

The storehouse is represented as matrix n × m. Every sofa takes two neighbouring by some side cells. No cell is covered by more than one sofa. There can be empty cells.

Sofa A is standing to the left of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that x_{a} < x_{b}, a is covered by A and b is covered by B. Sofa A is standing to the top of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that y_{a} < y_{b}, a is covered by A and b is covered by B. Right and bottom conditions are declared the same way. 

Note that in all conditions A ≠ B. Also some sofa A can be both to the top of another sofa B and to the bottom of it. The same is for left and right conditions.

The note also stated that there are cnt_{l} sofas to the left of Grandpa Maks's sofa, cnt_{r} — to the right, cnt_{t} — to the top and cnt_{b} — to the bottom.

Grandpa Maks asks you to help him to identify his sofa. It is guaranteed that there is no more than one sofa of given conditions.

Output the number of Grandpa Maks's sofa. If there is no such sofa that all the conditions are met for it then output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer number d (1 ≤ d ≤ 10^5) — the number of sofas in the storehouse.

The second line contains two integer numbers n, m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^5) — the size of the storehouse.

Next d lines contains four integer numbers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 (1 ≤ x_1, x_2 ≤ n, 1 ≤ y_1, y_2 ≤ m) — coordinates of the i-th sofa. It is guaranteed that cells (x_1, y_1) and (x_2, y_2) have common side, (x_1, y_1)  ≠  (x_2, y_2) and no cell is covered by more than one sofa.

The last line contains four integer numbers cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} (0 ≤ cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} ≤ d - 1).


-----Output-----

Print the number of the sofa for which all the conditions are met. Sofas are numbered 1 through d as given in input. If there is no such sofa then print -1.


-----Examples-----
Input
2
3 2
3 1 3 2
1 2 2 2
1 0 0 1

Output
1

Input
3
10 10
1 2 1 1
5 5 6 5
6 4 5 4
2 1 2 0

Output
2

Input
2
2 2
2 1 1 1
1 2 2 2
1 0 0 0

Output
-1



-----Note-----

Let's consider the second example.   The first sofa has 0 to its left, 2 sofas to its right ((1, 1) is to the left of both (5, 5) and (5, 4)), 0 to its top and 2 to its bottom (both 2nd and 3rd sofas are below).  The second sofa has cnt_{l} = 2, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 2 and cnt_{b} = 0.  The third sofa has cnt_{l} = 2, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 1 and cnt_{b} = 1. 

So the second one corresponds to the given conditions.

In the third example   The first sofa has cnt_{l} = 1, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 0 and cnt_{b} = 1.  The second sofa has cnt_{l} = 1, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 1 and cnt_{b} = 0.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a storehouse represented as an n×m grid
2. Each sofa occupies two adjacent cells (sharing a side)
3. We need to find which sofa has exactly `cnt_l` sofas to its left, `cnt_r` to its right, `cnt_t` to its top, and `cnt_b` to its bottom
4. A sofa A is to the left of sofa B if there's a cell in A with smaller x-coordinate than any cell in B
5. Similarly for top, right, and bottom

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Understand the conditions
- Sofa A is to the left of sofa B if min(x_coord of A) < max(x_coord of B) AND there's at least one cell in A with x < some cell in B
- Actually, simpler: A is to the left of B if there exists a cell a in A and cell b in B such that x_a < x_b
- This means: sofa A is to the left of sofa B if the minimum x-coordinate of A is less than the maximum x-coordinate of B? Not exactly.

Let me reconsider: A is to the left of B if there exists cell a in A and cell b in B such that x_a < x_b. This is equivalent to: min(x_coords of A) < max(x_coords of B) - but only if there's a cell in A with x < some cell in B.

Actually, it's simpler: A is to the left of B if there's any cell in A with x-coordinate strictly less than any cell in B's x-coordinates. More precisely:
- A is to the left of B if min(x of cells in A) < max(x of cells in B)

Wait, that's not right either. Let me think again:
- A is to the left of B if there exists cell a in A and cell b in B such that x_a < x_b
- This means the minimum x-coordinate of A must be less than the maximum x-coordinate of B? Not necessarily.

Actually, if there's any cell in A with x-coordinate less than any cell in B's x-coordinate, then A is to the left of B.

The condition is satisfied if min(x for A) < max(x for B)? No. 

For example: A has cells at (1,1) and (2,1), B has cells at (3,1) and (4,1). Then A is to the left of B because x=1 < x=3.

If A has cells at (3,1) and (4,1), B has (1,1) and (2,1), then A is NOT to the left of B.

So A is to the left of B iff min(x coords of A) < max(x coords of B) and there's at least one cell in A with x < max(x coords of B) - actually, if min(x of A) < max(x of B), then there's at least one cell in A with x < max(x of B), so there's at least one cell a in A and b in B such that x_a < x_b.

Actually, if min(x of A) < max(x of B), then we can pick a cell in A with x = min(x of A) and a cell in B with x = max(x of B), and x_a < x_b.

But what if min(x of A) >= max(x of B)? Then for all cells in A, x_a >= max(x of B), so for all cells a in A and b in B, x_a >= x_b, so no cell in A has x < x_b, so A is NOT to the left of B.

So: A is to the left of B iff min(x_coords of A) < max(x_coords of B).

Similarly:
- A is to the top of B iff min(y_coords of A) < max(y_coords of B)
- A is to the right of B iff max(x_coords of A) > min(x_coords of B)
- A is to the bottom of B iff max(y_coords of A) > min(y_coords of B)

### Step 2: Algorithm

1. For each sofa i, calculate:
   - min_x_i = min(x1, x2) of sofa i
   - max_x_i = max(x1, x2) of sofa i  
   - min_y_i = min(y1, y2) of sofa i
   - max_y_i = max(y1, y2) of sofa i

2. For each sofa i, count:
   - cnt_l_i: number of sofas j such that min_x_j < max_x_i
   - cnt_r_i: number of sofas j such that max_x_j > min_x_i
   - cnt_t_i: number of sofas j such that min_y_j < max_y_i
   - cnt_b_i: number of sofas j such that max_y_j > min_y_i

3. Check if any sofa i has (cnt_l_i, cnt_r_i, cnt_t_i, cnt_b_i) = (cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b)

4. Return the sofa number (1-indexed) if found, otherwise -1

### Step 3: Implementation

```python
d = int(input())
n, m = map(int, input().split())

sofas = []
for i in range(d):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
    sofas.append((x1, y1, x2, y2))

cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b = map(int, input().split())

# Precompute min and max coordinates for each sofa
min_x = []
max_x = []
min_y = []
max_y = []

for i in range(d):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = sofas[i]
    min_x.append(min(x1, x2))
    max_x.append(max(x1, x2))
    min_y.append(min(y1, y2))
    max_y.append(max(y1, y2))

# For each sofa, count how many sofas are to its left, right, top, bottom
result = -1

for i in range(d):
    # Count sofas to the left of sofa i
    cnt_l_i = 0
    for j in range(d):
        if i != j and min_x[j] < max_x[i]:
            cnt_l_i += 1
    
    # Count sofas to the right of sofa i
    cnt_r_i = 0
    for j in range(d):
        if i != j and max_x[j] > min_x[i]:
            cnt_r_i += 1
    
    # Count sofas to the top of sofa i
    cnt_t_i = 0
    for j in range(d):
        if i != j and min_y[j] < max_y[i]:
            cnt_t_i += 1
    
    # Count sofas to the bottom of sofa i
    cnt_b_i = 0
    for j in range(d):
        if i != j and max_y[j] > min_y[i]:
            cnt_b_i += 1
    
    if (cnt_l_i == cnt_l and cnt_r_i == cnt_r and cnt_t_i == cnt_t and cnt_b_i == cnt_b):
        result = i + 1
        break

print(result)
```

Wait, this O(d²) solution might be too slow for d = 10^5. Let me optimize it.

### Step 4: Optimization

We can sort the sofas by their x and y coordinates and use binary search.

For each sofa i, we need to count:
- How many sofas j have min_x[j] < max_x[i] (sofas to the left of i)
- How many sofas j have max_x[j] > min_x[i] (sofas to the right of i)
- How many sofas j have min_y[j] < max_y[i] (sofas to the top of i)
- How many sofas j have max_y[j] > min_y[i] (sofas to the bottom of i)

We can pre-sort and use binary search:

```python
d = int(input())
n, m = map(int, input().split())

sofas = []
for i in range(d):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
    sofas.append((x1, y1, x2, y2))

cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b = map(int, input().split())

# Precompute min and max coordinates for each sofa
min_x = []
max_x = []
min_y = []
max_y = []

for i in range(d):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = sofas[i]
    min_x.append(min(x1, x2))
    max_x.append(max(x1, x2))
    min_y.append(min(y1, y2))
    max_y.append(max(y1, y2))

# Create sorted arrays for efficient counting
sorted_min_x = sorted(min_x)
sorted_max_x = sorted(max_x)
sorted_min_y = sorted(min_y)
sorted_max_y = sorted(max_y)

result = -1

for i in range(d):
    # Count sofas to the left of sofa i: min_x[j] < max_x[i]
    # Find the count of elements in sorted_min_x that are < max_x[i]
    left_count = 0
    for j in range(len(sorted_min_x)):
        if sorted_min_x[j] < max_x[i]:
            left_count += 1
        else:
            break
    
    # Count sofas to the right of sofa i: max_x[j] > min_x[i]
    right_count = 0
    for j in range(len(sorted_max_x)):
        if sorted_max_x[j] > min_x[i]:
            right_count += 1
        else:
            break
    
    # Count sofas to the top of sofa i: min_y[j] < max_y[i]
    top_count = 0
    for j in range(len(sorted_min_y)):
        if sorted_min_y[j] < max_y[i]:
            top_count += 1
        else:
            break
    
    # Count sofas to the bottom of sofa i: max_y[j] > min_y[i]
    bottom_count = 0
    for j in range(len(sorted_max_y)):
        if sorted_max_y[j] > min_y[i]:
            bottom_count += 1
        else:
            break
    
    if (left_count == cnt_l and right_count == cnt_r and top_count == cnt_t and bottom_count == cnt_b):
        result = i + 1
        break

print(result)
```

Actually, the sorting approach isn't quite right because we need to find elements strictly less than a value. Let me use bisect:

```python

# Precompute min and max coordinates for each sofa

max_x = []
min_y = []
max_y = []

for i in range(d):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = sofas[i]
    min_x.append(min(x1, x2))
    max_x.append(max(x1, x2))
    min_y.append(min(y1, y2))
    max_y.append(max(y1, y2))

# Create sorted arrays
sorted_min_x = sorted(min_x)
sorted_max_x = sorted(max_x)
sorted_min_y = sorted(min_y)
sorted_max_y = sorted(max_y)

result = -1

for i in range(d):
    # Count sofas to the left of sofa i: min_x[j] < max_x[i]
    # Find the rightmost position where min_x[j] < max_x[i]
    pos = bisect.bisect_left(sorted_min_x, max_x[i])
    left_count = pos
    
    # Count sofas to the right of sofa i: max_x[j] > min_x[i]
    pos = bisect.bisect_right(sorted_max_x, min_x[i])
    right_count = d - pos
    
    # Count sofas to the top of sofa i: min_y[j] < max_y[i]
    pos = bisect.bisect_left(sorted_min_y, max_y[i])
    top_count = pos
    
    # Count sofas to the bottom of sofa i: max_y[j] > min_y[i]
    pos = bisect.bisect_right(sorted_max_y, min_y[i])
    bottom_count = d - pos
    
    if (left_count == cnt_l and right_count == cnt_r and top_count == cnt_t and bottom_count == cnt_b):
        result = i + 1
        break

print(result)
```

```python
min_y = []
max_y = []

    max_x.append(max(x1, x2))
    min_y.append(min(y1, y2))

sorted_max_x = sorted(max_x)

sorted_max_y = sorted(max_y)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Yet another round on DecoForces is coming! Grandpa Maks wanted to participate in it but someone has stolen his precious sofa! And how can one perform well with such a major loss?

Fortunately, the thief had left a note for Grandpa Maks. This note got Maks to the sofa storehouse. Still he had no idea which sofa belongs to him as they all looked the same!

The storehouse is represented as matrix n × m. Every sofa takes two neighbouring by some side cells. No cell is covered by more than one sofa. There can be empty cells.

Sofa A is standing to the left of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that x_{a} < x_{b}, a is covered by A and b is covered by B. Sofa A is standing to the top of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that y_{a} < y_{b}, a is covered by A and b is covered by B. Right and bottom conditions are declared the same way. 

Note that in all conditions A ≠ B. Also some sofa A can be both to the top of another sofa B and to the bottom of it. The same is for left and right conditions.

The note also stated that there are cnt_{l} sofas to the left of Grandpa Maks's sofa, cnt_{r} — to the right, cnt_{t} — to the top and cnt_{b} — to the bottom.

Grandpa Maks asks you to help him to identify his sofa. It is guaranteed that there is no more than one sofa of given conditions.

Output the number of Grandpa Maks's sofa. If there is no such sofa that all the conditions are met for it then output -1.


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer number d (1 ≤ d ≤ 10^5) — the number of sofas in the storehouse.

The second line contains two integer numbers n, m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 10^5) — the size of the storehouse.

Next d lines contains four integer numbers x_1, y_1, x_2, y_2 (1 ≤ x_1, x_2 ≤ n, 1 ≤ y_1, y_2 ≤ m) — coordinates of the i-th sofa. It is guaranteed that cells (x_1, y_1) and (x_2, y_2) have common side, (x_1, y_1)  ≠  (x_2, y_2) and no cell is covered by more than one sofa.

The last line contains four integer numbers cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} (0 ≤ cnt_{l}, cnt_{r}, cnt_{t}, cnt_{b} ≤ d - 1).


-----Output-----

Print the number of the sofa for which all the conditions are met. Sofas are numbered 1 through d as given in input. If there is no such sofa then print -1.


-----Examples-----
Input
2
3 2
3 1 3 2
1 2 2 2
1 0 0 1

Output
1

Input
3
10 10
1 2 1 1
5 5 6 5
6 4 5 4
2 1 2 0

Output
2

Input
2
2 2
2 1 1 1
1 2 2 2
1 0 0 0

Output
-1



-----Note-----

Let's consider the second example.   The first sofa has 0 to its left, 2 sofas to its right ((1, 1) is to the left of both (5, 5) and (5, 4)), 0 to its top and 2 to its bottom (both 2nd and 3rd sofas are below).  The second sofa has cnt_{l} = 2, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 2 and cnt_{b} = 0.  The third sofa has cnt_{l} = 2, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 1 and cnt_{b} = 1. 

So the second one corresponds to the given conditions.

In the third example   The first sofa has cnt_{l} = 1, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 0 and cnt_{b} = 1.  The second sofa has cnt_{l} = 1, cnt_{r} = 1, cnt_{t} = 1 and cnt_{b} = 0.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a storehouse of size n×m with d sofas, each occupying two adjacent cells
2. A sofa A is to the left of sofa B if there's a cell in A with smaller x-coordinate than a cell in B
3. Similarly for top/bottom/right conditions
4. We need to find which sofa has exactly cnt_l sofas to its left, cnt_r to its right, cnt_t to its top, and cnt_b to its bottom
5. If no such sofa exists, return -1

## Key Insights

1. For each sofa, I need to count how many other sofas are to its left, right, top, and bottom
2. Since we have coordinates of both cells of each sofa, I can check all possible pairs
3. For a sofa to have another sofa to its left: there must be a cell in the first sofa with smaller x-coordinate than a cell in the second sofa
4. Similarly for other directions

## Algorithm Steps

1. Read input: number of sofas, storehouse dimensions, sofa coordinates, and counts
2. For each sofa, calculate how many other sofas are to its left, right, top, and bottom
3. Compare these counts with the given cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b
4. Return the sofa number if exactly matching, otherwise -1

## Implementation Strategy

For each sofa i and other sofa j:
- Check if sofa i is to the left of sofa j: min(x_i) < max(x_j) AND there exists a cell in i with x < a cell in j
- Actually, simpler: sofa i is to the left of sofa j if there exists a cell in i with x < a cell in j
- This means: min(x_coords of i) < max(x_coords of j) AND min(x_coords of i) < some x in j

Wait, let me reconsider: 
Sofa A is to the left of sofa B if there exist cells a in A and b in B such that x_a < x_b.

So for sofa A, it has a cell with x-coordinate x_a, and for sofa B it has a cell with x-coordinate x_b, and x_a < x_b.

This means: if sofa A has any cell with x-coordinate less than any cell in sofa B, then A is to the left of B.

For sofa A to have a sofa B to its left: A has a cell with x < some cell in B.

So for a given sofa i, to count how many sofas j are to its left (meaning j is to the left of i):
- Check if there's any cell in j with x-coordinate < some cell in i

For a sofa i to have k sofas to its left: there are exactly k other sofas j such that min(x_coords of j) < max(x_coords of i) and min(x_coords of j) < max(x_coords of i) - actually not exactly.

Actually: sofa j is to the left of sofa i if there exists cell j1 in j and cell i1 in i such that x_j1 < x_i1.

This means: if min(x_coords of j) < max(x_coords of i), then j is to the left of i.

But it's not sufficient - what if j has only high x-coordinates? Actually, if min(x_j) < max(x_i), then we can pick x_j and x_i where x_j < x_i.

For any two sets of coordinates, if min(set1) < max(set2), then there exist elements where one is less than the other.

Actually, if min(j) < max(i), then we can find x_j in j and x_i in i such that x_j < x_i.

Therefore, sofa j is to the left of sofa i if and only if min(x_coords of j) < max(x_coords of i).

Similarly:
- j is to the right of i if max(x_coords of j) > min(x_coords of i)
- j is to the top of i if min(y_coords of j) < max(y_coords of i)
- j is to the bottom of i if max(y_coords of j) > min(y_coords of i)

Wait, let me verify:
For left: j is to the left of i if there exists x_j < x_i
- This is true if min(x_j) < max(x_i) (since we can pick the smallest x in j and largest x in i)

For right: j is to the right of i if there exists x_j > x_i
- This is true if max(x_j) > min(x_i)

For top: j is to the top of i if there exists y_j < y_i
- This is true if min(y_j) < max(y_i)

For bottom: j is to the bottom of i if there exists y_j > y_i  
- This is true if max(y_j) > min(y_i)

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
d = int(input())
n, m = map(int, input().split())

sofas = []
for i in range(d):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
    # Store both x coordinates and both y coordinates for each sofa
    sofas.append((min(x1, x2), max(x1, x2), min(y1, y2), max(y1, y2)))

cnt_l, cnt_r, cnt_t, cnt_b = map(int, input().split())

# For each sofa, count how many others are to its left, right, top, bottom
result = -1

for i in range(d):
    left_count = 0
    right_count = 0
    top_count = 0
    bottom_count = 0
    
    # Check all other sofas j
    for j in range(d):
        if i == j:
            continue
            
        x1i, x2i, y1i, y2i = sofas[i]
        x1j, x2j, y1j, y2j = sofas[j]
        
        # Check if j is to the left of i (exists x_j < x_i)
        if min(x1j, x2j) < max(x1i, x2i):
            left_count += 1
            
        # Check if j is to the right of i (exists x_j > x_i)
        if max(x1j, x2j) > min(x1i, x2i):
            right_count += 1
            
        # Check if j is to the top of i (exists y_j < y_i)
        if min(y1j, y2j) < max(y1i, y2i):
            top_count += 1
            
        # Check if j is to the bottom of i (exists y_j > y_i)
        if max(y1j, y2j) > min(y1i, y2i):
            bottom_count += 1
    
    # Check if this sofa matches the required counts
    if left_count == cnt_l and right_count == cnt_r and top_count == cnt_t and bottom_count == cnt_b:
        result = i + 1  # Sofa numbers are 1-indexed
        break

print(result)
```

Wait, I think I reversed the logic. Let me check the problem again.

The problem says: "Sofa A is standing to the left of sofa B if there exist two such cells a and b that x_a < x_b, a is covered by A and b is covered by B."

So if A is to the left of B, then x_a < x_b.

For a given sofa i, how many sofas j are to its left? This means j is to the left of i, so there exists cell in j with x-coordinate < cell in i.

So j is to the left of i if min(x_coords of j) < max(x_coords of i).

For sofa i, how many sofas j are to its right? j is to the right of i if max(x_coords of j) > min(x_coords of i).

Similarly for top and bottom.

This matches what I have above.

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1:
- Sofa 1: (3,1) and (3,2) → x=[3,3], y=[1,2]
- Sofa 2: (1,2) and (2,2) → x=[1,2], y=[2,2]

For sofa 1:
- Count left: j=2 has min(x)=1 < max(x of 1)=3 → yes, so 1 left
- Count right: j=2 has max(x)=2, min(x of 1)=3 → 2 < 3 → no right
- Count top: j=2 has min(y)=2, max(y of 1)=2 → 2 < 2? No → 0 top
- Count bottom: j=2 has max(y)=2, min(y of 1)=1 → 2 > 1 → yes → 1 bottom

But expected is cnt_l=1, cnt_r=0, cnt_t=0, cnt_b=1, which matches the input (1,0,0,1).

For sofa 2:
- Count left: j=1 has min(x)=3, max(x of 2)=2 → 3 < 2? No → 0 left
- Count right: j=1 has max(x)=3, min(x of 2)=1 → 3 > 1 → yes → 1 right
- Count top: j=1 has min(y)=1, max(y of 2)=2 → 1 < 2 → yes → 1 top
- Count bottom: j=1 has max(y)=2, min(y of 2)=2 → 2 > 2? No → 0 bottom

This gives (0,1,1,0) which doesn't match (1,0,0,1).

So sofa 1 has (1,0,0,1) which matches.

The input says output should be 1, which is correct.

Example 2:
- Sofa 1: (1,2) to (1,1) → x=[1,1], y=[1,2]
- Sofa 2: (5,5) to (6,5) → x=[5,6], y=[5,5]
- Sofa 3: (6,4) to (5,4) → x=[5,6], y=[4,4]

For sofa 2:
- Count left: j=1: min(x)=1 < max(x of 2)=6 → yes; j=3: min(x)=5 < 6 → yes → 2 left
- Count right: j=1: max(x)=1, min(x of 2)=5 → 1 > 5? No; j=3: max(x)=6, min(x of 2)=5 → 6 > 5 → yes → 1 right
- Count top: j=1: min(y)=1 < max(y of 2)=5 → yes; j=3: min(y)=4 < 5 → yes → 2 top
- Count bottom: j=1: max(y)=2, min(y of 2)=5 → 2 > 5? No; j=3: max(y)=4, min(y of 2)=5 → 4 > 5? No → 0 bottom

This gives (2,1,2,0) which matches the input (2,1,2,0). So sofa 2 is correct.

```python
        if min(y1j, y2j) < max(y1i, y2i):
            top_count += 1
            
        # Check if j is to the bottom of i (exists cell in j with y > cell in i)
        if max(y1j, y2j) > min(y1i, y2i):
            bottom_count += 1
    
    # Check if this sofa matches the required counts
    if left_count == cnt_l and right_count == cnt_r and top_count == cnt_t and bottom_count == cnt_b:
        result = i + 1  # Sofa numbers are 1-indexed
        break

print(result)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

As you probably know, Anton goes to school. One of the school subjects that Anton studies is Bracketology. On the Bracketology lessons students usually learn different sequences that consist of round brackets (characters "(" and ")" (without quotes)).

On the last lesson Anton learned about the regular simple bracket sequences (RSBS). A bracket sequence s of length n is an RSBS if the following conditions are met:

  * It is not empty (that is n ≠ 0). 
  * The length of the sequence is even. 
  * First <image> charactes of the sequence are equal to "(". 
  * Last <image> charactes of the sequence are equal to ")". 



For example, the sequence "((()))" is an RSBS but the sequences "((())" and "(()())" are not RSBS.

Elena Ivanovna, Anton's teacher, gave him the following task as a homework. Given a bracket sequence s. Find the number of its distinct subsequences such that they are RSBS. Note that a subsequence of s is a string that can be obtained from s by deleting some of its elements. Two subsequences are considered distinct if distinct sets of positions are deleted.

Because the answer can be very big and Anton's teacher doesn't like big numbers, she asks Anton to find the answer modulo 109 + 7.

Anton thought of this task for a very long time, but he still doesn't know how to solve it. Help Anton to solve this task and write a program that finds the answer for it!

Input

The only line of the input contains a string s — the bracket sequence given in Anton's homework. The string consists only of characters "(" and ")" (without quotes). It's guaranteed that the string is not empty and its length doesn't exceed 200 000.

Output

Output one number — the answer for the task modulo 109 + 7.

Examples

Input

)(()()


Output

6


Input

)))

0

Note

In the first sample the following subsequences are possible:

  * If we delete characters at the positions 1 and 5 (numbering starts with one), we will get the subsequence "(())". 
  * If we delete characters at the positions 1, 2, 3 and 4, we will get the subsequence "()". 
  * If we delete characters at the positions 1, 2, 4 and 5, we will get the subsequence "()". 
  * If we delete characters at the positions 1, 2, 5 and 6, we will get the subsequence "()". 
  * If we delete characters at the positions 1, 3, 4 and 5, we will get the subsequence "()". 
  * If we delete characters at the positions 1, 3, 5 and 6, we will get the subsequence "()". 



The rest of the subsequnces are not RSBS. So we got 6 distinct subsequences that are RSBS, so the answer is 6.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the number of distinct subsequences that are RSBS (Regular Simple Bracket Sequences)
2. An RSBS has length n where n is even, first n/2 characters are "(", and last n/2 characters are ")"
3. A subsequence is formed by deleting some characters from the original string
4. We need to count distinct subsequences (different sets of positions deleted)

## Key Insights

1. For a subsequence to be RSBS, it must have exactly k opening brackets followed by exactly k closing brackets for some k > 0
2. We need to count all ways to select k opening brackets from available "(" characters and k closing brackets from available ")" characters
3. The key constraint is that in the resulting subsequence, all opening brackets must come before all closing brackets

## Algorithm Design

1. Let's say we have `open_count` opening brackets and `close_count` closing brackets in the original string
2. For each possible length k (from 1 to min(open_count, close_count)), we need to count:
   - Ways to choose k opening brackets from `open_count` available ones
   - Ways to choose k closing brackets from `close_count` available ones
3. The total number of valid RSBS subsequences is the sum over all k of (C(open_count, k) * C(close_count, k))

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Count total number of '(' and ')' characters
2. For each possible k from 1 to min(open_count, close_count):
   - Calculate combinations C(open_count, k) * C(close_count, k)
3. Sum all these products and return modulo 10^9 + 7

## Implementation Details

We need to compute binomial coefficients efficiently. Since the maximum length is 200,000, we can use dynamic programming or iterative calculation for combinations.

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 10**9 + 7
    
    s = input().strip()
    n = len(s)
    
    # Count opening and closing brackets
    open_count = s.count('(')
    close_count = s.count(')')
    
    # If we don't have at least one of each, no RSBS possible
    if open_count == 0 or close_count == 0:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Calculate binomial coefficients using dynamic programming
    # We need C(n, k) for various values
    
    # We only need combinations up to min(open_count, close_count)
    max_k = min(open_count, close_count)
    
    # Precompute factorials and inverse factorials up to max_k
    max_n = max(open_count, close_count)
    
    # Precompute factorials up to max_n
    fact = [1] * (max_n + 1)
    for i in range(1, max_n + 1):
        fact[i] = (fact[i-1] * i) % MOD
    
    # Precompute modular inverse using Fermat's little theorem
    def mod_inverse(a, mod):
        return pow(a, mod - 2, mod)
    
    # Precompute inverse factorials
    inv_fact = [1] * (max_n + 1)
    inv_fact[max_n] = mod_inverse(fact[max_n], MOD)
    for i in range(max_n - 1, -1, -1):
        inv_fact[i] = (inv_fact[i + 1] * (i + 1)) % MOD
    
    # Function to compute C(n, k)
    def combination(n, k):
        if k < 0 or k > n:
            return 0
        return (fact[n] * inv_fact[k] % MOD) * inv_fact[n - k] % MOD
    
    # Sum over all valid k values
    result = 0
    for k in range(1, max_k + 1):
        ways_open = combination(open_count, k)
        ways_close = combination(close_count, k)
        result = (result + (ways_open * ways_close) % MOD) % MOD
    
    print(result)

solve()
```

The algorithm works as follows:

1. **Input parsing**: Read the bracket sequence and count opening and closing brackets
2. **Edge case**: If we don't have both types of brackets, answer is 0
3. **Precomputation**: Calculate factorials and their modular inverses up to the maximum count needed
4. **Combination calculation**: For each possible k (length of RSBS half), calculate C(open_count, k) * C(close_count, k)
5. **Summation**: Add all valid combinations and return the result modulo 10^9 + 7

Time complexity: O(n + min(open_count, close_count) * log(max_n)) where n is the length of string
Space complexity: O(max_n) for storing factorials and inverse factorials

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the number of distinct subsequences that are RSBS (Regular Simple Bracket Sequences)
2. An RSBS has:
   - Length > 0 and even
   - First half consists of only '('
   - Second half consists of only ')'
3. A subsequence is formed by deleting some characters from the original string
4. We count distinct subsequences (different sets of positions deleted)

## Key Insights

1. For a subsequence to be RSBS of length 2k, we need exactly k opening brackets '(' and k closing brackets ')' 
2. The first k positions in the subsequence must be '(' and the last k positions must be ')'
3. We can think of this as choosing k positions from available '(' brackets and k positions from available ')' brackets
4. However, we need to ensure that in the final subsequence, the first k characters are all '(' and the last k characters are all ')'

## Approach

1. Count the total number of ways to choose k opening brackets from available '(' and k closing brackets from available ')' for each valid k
2. For each valid k (from 1 to the minimum of total '(' and total ')'):
   - Count how many ways we can choose k '(' brackets
   - Count how many ways we can choose k ')' brackets
   - Multiply these two counts (since we're choosing independently)
   - Add to total result

3. The total number of subsequences of length 2k that form RSBS is: C(open_count, k) × C(close_count, k)

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Count total '(' and ')' characters
2. For each possible k (from 1 to min(open_count, close_count)):
   - Calculate combinations C(open_count, k) and C(close_count, k)
   - Multiply them and add to result
3. Return result modulo 10^9 + 7

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    MOD = 10**9 + 7
    
    # Count total opening and closing brackets
    open_count = s.count('(')
    close_count = s.count(')')
    
    # If we don't have at least one of each, no RSBS possible
    if open_count == 0 or close_count == 0:
        return 0
    
    # Precompute factorials and inverse factorials for combination calculation
    max_n = max(open_count, close_count)
    fact = [1] * (max_n + 1)
    for i in range(1, max_n + 1):
        fact[i] = (fact[i-1] * i) % MOD
    
    def mod_inverse(a, mod):
        return pow(a, mod - 2, mod)
    
    def combination(n, r):
        if r > n or r < 0:
            return 0
        if r == 0 or r == n:
            return 1
        # C(n,r) = n! / (r! * (n-r)!)
        numerator = fact[n]
        denominator = (fact[r] * fact[n-r]) % MOD
        return (numerator * mod_inverse(denominator, MOD)) % MOD
    
    result = 0
    # For each possible length of RSBS (2k)
    for k in range(1, min(open_count, close_count) + 1):
        ways_open = combination(open_count, k)
        ways_close = combination(close_count, k)
        result = (result + (ways_open * ways_close) % MOD) % MOD
    
    return result

print(solve())
```

Wait, I need to reconsider the problem. The key insight is that when we form a subsequence, the relative order of characters is preserved. But for an RSBS, we need exactly k '(' followed by k ')'.

Let me think again: for a subsequence to be an RSBS, we need to select k opening brackets and k closing brackets such that the k opening brackets come before the k closing brackets in the original string.

Actually, no - the subsequence maintains the original order, so if we select k '(' and k ')' positions, the resulting sequence will have the opening brackets in their original order and closing brackets in their original order. For it to be RSBS, we need the first k characters to be '(' and the last k to be ')', which means the k opening brackets must all come before the k closing brackets in the original string order.

But actually, since we're forming a subsequence by deleting elements, the relative order is preserved. If we select k opening brackets and k closing brackets, the resulting sequence will naturally have the opening brackets in their original order and closing brackets in their original order. 

For it to be RSBS, we need exactly k opening brackets followed by k closing brackets in the subsequence. This happens when all selected opening brackets come before all selected closing brackets in the original string.

So the answer is: for each k from 1 to min(open_count, close_count), count the number of ways to choose k opening brackets from the available ones and k closing brackets from the available ones, such that all chosen opening brackets come before all chosen closing brackets.

This is equivalent to: for each k, we can choose any k opening brackets and any k closing brackets, but with the constraint that in the original string, all chosen opening brackets come before all chosen closing brackets.

Actually, I think I'm overcomplicating. Since we're selecting characters in order, if we select any k opening brackets and any k closing brackets, the resulting sequence will have the opening brackets in their relative order and closing brackets in their relative order. For it to be RSBS, we need the k opening brackets to be positioned such that they all come before the k closing brackets in the subsequence.

But since the subsequence maintains order, if we pick k opening brackets and k closing brackets, the resulting sequence will have the opening brackets in their original order and closing brackets in their original order. The condition that it forms RSBS means that there must be exactly k opening brackets followed by k closing brackets.

This happens when the k opening brackets are selected from the first part (before some position) and k closing brackets are selected from the second part (after that position).

Actually, I think the simpler interpretation is correct: for any selection of k opening brackets and k closing brackets, if we form the subsequence by keeping those positions in order, we get a sequence of k opening brackets followed by k closing brackets - but only if all the opening brackets are before all the closing brackets in the original string.

So for each k, we need to count valid combinations where all chosen opening brackets come before all chosen closing brackets.

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    MOD = 10**9 + 7
    
    # Count total opening and closing brackets
    open_count = s.count('(')
    close_count = s.count(')')
    
    if open_count == 0 or close_count == 0:
        return 0
    
    # Precompute factorials and inverse factorials for combination calculation
    max_n = max(open_count, close_count)
    fact = [1] * (max_n + 1)
    for i in range(1, max_n + 1):
        fact[i] = (fact[i-1] * i) % MOD
    
    def mod_inverse(a, mod):
        return pow(a, mod - 2, mod)
    
    def combination(n, r):
        if r > n or r < 0:
            return 0
        if r == 0 or r == n:
            return 1
        # C(n,r) = n! / (r! * (n-r)!)
        numerator = fact[n]
        denominator = (fact[r] * fact[n-r]) % MOD
        return (numerator * mod_inverse(denominator, MOD)) % MOD
    
    result = 0
    # For each possible k (number of opening brackets in RSBS)
    for k in range(1, min(open_count, close_count) + 1):
        # For each k, we can choose any k opening brackets and any k closing brackets
        ways_open = combination(open_count, k)
        ways_close = combination(close_count, k)
        result = (result + (ways_open * ways_close) % MOD) % MOD
    
    return result

print(solve())
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the number of distinct subsequences that are RSBS (Regular Simple Bracket Sequences)
2. An RSBS has:
   - Even length
   - First half consists of all "(" characters
   - Second half consists of all ")" characters
3. A subsequence is formed by deleting some elements from the original string
4. We need to count distinct subsequences (based on positions deleted)

## Key Insight

For a subsequence to be an RSBS of length 2k:
- It must have exactly k opening brackets "("
- It must have exactly k closing brackets ")"
- All opening brackets must come before all closing brackets in the subsequence
- This means we need to choose k positions from the available "(" characters and k positions from the available ")" characters

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Identify available positions**: We need to find all positions of '(' and ')' in the original string.

2. **For each possible length**: For a valid RSBS of length 2k, we need:
   - k positions that contain '(' from the original string
   - k positions that contain ')' from the original string
   - The k '(' positions must come before the k ')' positions in the subsequence

3. **Count valid combinations**: For each k, we need to count how many ways we can choose k '(' positions and k ')' positions such that in the resulting subsequence, all '(' come before all ')'.

4. **Important observation**: Since we're forming a subsequence, the relative order of characters is preserved. So if we pick k '(' from positions i₁ < i₂ < ... < iₖ and k ')' from positions j₁ < j₂ < ... < jₖ, the subsequence will be valid only if the largest '(' position is less than the smallest ')' position.

5. **Better approach**: For each possible k, we can:
   - Count the number of ways to choose k '(' from available '(' positions
   - Count the number of ways to choose k ')' from available ')' positions
   - But ensure that the k '(' positions are all before the k ')' positions

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, since we're forming a subsequence by preserving the original order, we just need to ensure that if we pick k '(' and k ')', then in the original string, the last picked '(' position comes before the first picked ')' position.

But there's a simpler way: For each possible k, we can consider all ways to choose k '(' positions and k ')' positions such that max(opening_positions) < min(closing_positions).

However, there's an even better insight: we can use dynamic programming or combinatorial counting.

Let me think differently. For each possible length 2k, we can:
1. Choose k positions from the '(' positions
2. Choose k positions from the ')' positions  
3. Ensure that the last position of the selected '(' is less than the first position of the selected ')'

## Algorithm

1. Count the total number of ways to form RSBS by considering all valid k values
2. For each k, iterate through all possible splits of the string
3. Use prefix sums to efficiently count valid combinations

Actually, let me implement a cleaner solution:

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    n = len(s)
    MOD = 10**9 + 7
    
    # Count total number of valid RSBS subsequences
    total = 0
    
    # For each possible k (length of RSBS is 2k)
    # We need to choose k opening brackets from available '(' and k closing brackets from available ')'
    # such that all selected opening brackets come before all selected closing brackets
    
    # Get positions of '(' and ')' 
    open_positions = []
    close_positions = []
    
    for i in range(n):
        if s[i] == '(':
            open_positions.append(i)
        else:
            close_positions.append(i)
    
    # If we don't have enough opening or closing brackets, return 0
    if len(open_positions) == 0 or len(close_positions) == 0:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # For each possible k from 1 to min(len(open_positions), len(close_positions))
    for k in range(1, min(len(open_positions), len(close_positions)) + 1):
        # For each possible split point where all k opening brackets come before all k closing brackets
        for split in range(n):
            # Count opening brackets that are before split position
            open_before = 0
            close_after = 0
            
            # Count how many opening brackets we can choose from positions < split
            # and how many closing brackets we can choose from positions > split
            for i in open_positions:
                if i < split:
                    open_before += 1
            for i in close_positions:
                if i > split:
                    close_after += 1
            
            # We can form RSBS of length 2k if we have at least k opening brackets before split
            # and at least k closing brackets after split
            if open_before >= k and close_after >= k:
                # Ways to choose k positions from open_before opening brackets
                # Ways to choose k positions from close_after closing brackets
                # Using combinatorics formula C(open_before, k) * C(close_after, k)
                
                # We need to calculate combinations efficiently
                # For now, we'll use a simplified approach with precomputation
                
                # This is getting complex. Let me implement a different approach.
                pass
    
    # Let me implement a much simpler and correct approach
    
    # For each possible RSBS length 2k, we need to count valid subsequences
    # A valid subsequence has k opening and k closing brackets, and all opening come before all closing
    
    # We can do this by considering each possible k and each possible split point
    result = 0
    
    # For each k from 1 to min(len(open_positions), len(close_positions))
    k_max = min(len(open_positions), len(close_positions))
    
    for k in range(1, k_max + 1):
        # For each possible split position
        # The split position divides the string into left (before) and right (after)
        # We need to pick k opening brackets from left part and k closing brackets from right part
        
        # Go through each possible split position from 0 to n
        for split in range(n):
            # Count opening brackets in left part (positions < split)
            left_opens = 0
            for pos in open_positions:
                if pos < split:
                    left_opens += 1
            
            # Count closing brackets in right part (positions > split)
            right_closes = 0
            for pos in close_positions:
                if pos > split:
                    right_closes += 1
            
            # We can form valid RSBS of length 2k if we have at least k opening brackets in left
            # and at least k closing brackets in right
            if left_opens >= k and right_closes >= k:
                # Calculate C(left_opens, k) * C(right_closes, k)
                # We need to compute combinations efficiently
                
                # Calculate combination C(n, r) = n! / (r! * (n-r)!)
                # But we need to handle modular arithmetic
                
                # Since the constraints are up to 200,000, we can't precompute factorials for that
                # Let's implement a helper function for combinations
                
                # We'll use the fact that C(n, r) = C(n, n-r)
                # And C(n, r) = C(n-1, r-1) + C(n-1, r)
                
                # But calculating combinations directly might be slow
                # Let me use a different approach: iterate through all possible combinations
                
                # Actually, for each k and each split, we just need to count valid pairs
                # We can precompute combination values using dynamic programming
                
                # For now, let me implement the calculation of combinations
                pass
    
    # This is getting too complex. Let me implement a cleaner solution with proper combinatorics
    
    MOD = 10**9 + 7
    
    # Precompute factorials and inverse factorials up to n
    # But n can be up to 200,000
    max_n = 200000
    
    # Precompute factorials
    fact = [1] * (max_n + 1)
    for i in range(1, max_n + 1):
        fact[i] = (fact[i-1] * i) % MOD
    
    # Precompute inverse factorials
    inv_fact = [1] * (max_n + 1)
    inv_fact[max_n] = pow(fact[max_n], MOD - 2, MOD)
    for i in range(max_n - 1, -1, -1):
        inv_fact[i] = (inv_fact[i + 1] * (i + 1)) % MOD
    
    def comb(n, r):
        if r < 0 or r > n:
            return 0
        return (fact[n] * inv_fact[r] % MOD) * inv_fact[n - r] % MOD
    
    # Get positions of '(' and ')'
    open_positions = []
    close_positions = []
    
    for i in range(n):
        if s[i] == '(':
            open_positions.append(i)
        else:
            close_positions.append(i)
    
    # If we don't have enough brackets, return 0
    if len(open_positions) == 0 or len(close_positions) == 0:
        print(0)
        return
    
    total = 0
    k_max = min(len(open_positions), len(close_positions))
    
    # For each possible k (length 2k)
    for k in range(1, k_max + 1):
        # For each possible split point
        for split in range(n):
            # Count opening brackets before split
            left_opens = sum(1 for pos in open_positions if pos < split)
            # Count closing brackets after split
            right_closes = sum(1 for pos in close_positions if pos > split)
            
            if left_opens >= k and right_closes >= k:
                ways = comb(left_opens, k) * comb(right_closes, k) % MOD
                total = (total + ways) % MOD
    
    print(total)

solve()
```

Wait, this approach has O(n² * min(open, close)) complexity which might be too slow for n=200,000. Let me optimize it.

Actually, let me reconsider the problem. We can optimize by noticing that for a fixed k, we can calculate the contribution without iterating through every split.

But given the constraints and the fact that the expected solution might be simpler, let me implement the working solution with proper optimization:

```python
def solve():
    s = input().strip()
    n = len(s)
    MOD = 10**9 + 7
    
    # Precompute factorials and inverse factorials
    max_val = 200000
    fact = [1] * (max_val + 1)
    for i in range(1, max_val + 1):
        fact[i] = (fact[i-1] * i) % MOD
    
    inv_fact = [1] * (max_val + 1)
    inv_fact[max_val] = pow(fact[max_val], MOD - 2, MOD)
    for i in range(max_val - 1, -1, -1):
        inv_fact[i] = (inv_fact[i + 1] * (i + 1)) % MOD
    
    def nCr(n, r):
        if r < 0 or r > n:
            return 0
        return (fact[n] * inv_fact[r] % MOD) * inv_fact[n - r] % MOD
    
    # Get positions of opening and closing brackets
    open_positions = []
    close_positions = []
    
    for i, char in enumerate(s):
        if char == '(':
            open_positions.append(i)
        else:
            close_positions.append(i)
    
    # If we don't have both types of brackets, return 0
    if len(open_positions) == 0 or len(close_positions) == 0:
        print(0)
        return
    
    total = 0
    # For each possible k (number of opening brackets in RSBS)
    k_max = min(len(open_positions), len(close_positions))
    
    # For each k from 1 to k_max
    for k in range(1, k_max + 1):
        # For each possible split position
        # The split divides the string - all selected opening brackets must be before all selected closing brackets
        # So we can iterate through positions where split is the boundary
        
        # Instead of iterating through all splits, we can use the fact that we only need to consider
        # positions where we have enough opening brackets to the left and closing to the right
        
        # Calculate the contribution for each split
        for split_pos in range(n):
            # Count opening brackets before split_pos
            left_opens = 0
            for pos in open_positions:
                if pos < split_pos:
                    left_opens += 1
            
            # Count closing brackets after split_pos
            right_closes = 0
            for pos in close_positions:
                if pos > split_pos:
                    right_closes += 1
            
            if left_opens >= k and right_closes >= k:
                ways = nCr(left_opens, k) * nCr(right_closes, k) % MOD
                total = (total + ways) % MOD
    
    print(total)

solve()
```

This solution is correct but might be too slow. Let me check if there's a more efficient way.

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

As you probably know, Anton goes to school. One of the school subjects that Anton studies is Bracketology. On the Bracketology lessons students usually learn different sequences that consist of round brackets (characters "(" and ")" (without quotes)).

On the last lesson Anton learned about the regular simple bracket sequences (RSBS). A bracket sequence s of length n is an RSBS if the following conditions are met:

  * It is not empty (that is n ≠ 0). 
  * The length of the sequence is even. 
  * First <image> charactes of the sequence are equal to "(". 
  * Last <image> charactes of the sequence are equal to ")". 



For example, the sequence "((()))" is an RSBS but the sequences "((())" and "(()())" are not RSBS.

Elena Ivanovna, Anton's teacher, gave him the following task as a homework. Given a bracket sequence s. Find the number of its distinct subsequences such that they are RSBS. Note that a subsequence of s is a string that can be obtained from s by deleting some of its elements. Two subsequences are considered distinct if distinct sets of positions are deleted.

Because the answer can be very big and Anton's teacher doesn't like big numbers, she asks Anton to find the answer modulo 109 + 7.

Anton thought of this task for a very long time, but he still doesn't know how to solve it. Help Anton to solve this task and write a program that finds the answer for it!

Input

The only line of the input contains a string s — the bracket sequence given in Anton's homework. The string consists only of characters "(" and ")" (without quotes). It's guaranteed that the string is not empty and its length doesn't exceed 200 000.

Output

Output one number — the answer for the task modulo 109 + 7.

Examples

Input

)(()()


Output

6


Input

()()()


Output

7


Input

)))


Output

0

Note

In the first sample the following subsequences are possible:

  * If we delete characters at the positions 1 and 5 (numbering starts with one), we will get the subsequence "(())". 
  * If we delete characters at the positions 1, 2, 3 and 4, we will get the subsequence "()". 
  * If we delete characters at the positions 1, 2, 4 and 5, we will get the subsequence "()". 
  * If we delete characters at the positions 1, 2, 5 and 6, we will get the subsequence "()". 
  * If we delete characters at the positions 1, 3, 4 and 5, we will get the subsequence "()". 
  * If we delete characters at the positions 1, 3, 5 and 6, we will get the subsequence "()". 



The rest of the subsequnces are not RSBS. So we got 6 distinct subsequences that are RSBS, so the answer is 6.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the number of distinct subsequences that are RSBS (Regular Simple Bracket Sequences)
2. An RSBS has:
   - Length > 0 and even
   - First n/2 characters are "(" 
   - Last n/2 characters are ")"
3. A subsequence is formed by deleting some characters from the original string
4. We need to count distinct subsequences (based on which positions are deleted)

## Key Insights

For a subsequence to be an RSBS of length 2k:
- It must have exactly k opening brackets "(" at the beginning
- It must have exactly k closing brackets ")" at the end
- So we need to choose k positions from available "(" and k positions from available ")" 

## Approach

1. Count how many "(" and ")" characters we have in the original string
2. For each possible length 2k (where k ranges from 1 to min(count_open, count_close)):
   - We need to choose k positions from the available "(" characters
   - We need to choose k positions from the available ")" characters
   - The total ways = C(open_count, k) × C(close_count, k)

3. However, we need to be careful about the positions. The RSBS requires that the first k characters are "(", and the last k characters are ")". 

Wait, let me reconsider. The problem states that an RSBS has first n/2 characters as "(" and last n/2 as ")". This means in the subsequence, we must have exactly k opening brackets followed by k closing brackets.

So for each possible k (1 to min(open_count, close_count)):
- We need to select k positions from the original "(" characters
- We need to select k positions from the original ")" characters
- The subsequence will be formed by taking these 2k positions and arranging them in order of appearance

## Algorithm

1. Count total opening and closing brackets
2. For each k from 1 to min(open_count, close_count):
   - Calculate combinations C(open_count, k) × C(close_count, k)
3. Sum all these products

The key insight is that any valid selection of k opening brackets and k closing brackets will form a valid RSBS when arranged in order of appearance (since the opening brackets appear before their corresponding closing brackets).

```python
def solve():
    MOD = 10**9 + 7
    
    s = input().strip()
    n = len(s)
    
    # Count opening and closing brackets
    open_count = s.count('(')
    close_count = s.count(')')
    
    # If we don't have at least one of each, answer is 0
    if open_count == 0 or close_count == 0:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Precompute factorials and inverse factorials for combinations
    max_n = max(open_count, close_count)
    
    # Precompute factorials up to max_n
    fact = [1] * (max_n + 1)
    for i in range(1, max_n + 1):
        fact[i] = (fact[i-1] * i) % MOD
    
    # Precompute modular inverse using Fermat's little theorem
    def mod_inverse(a, mod):
        return pow(a, mod - 2, mod)
    
    inv_fact = [1] * (max_n + 1)
    inv_fact[max_n] = mod_inverse(fact[max_n], MOD)
    for i in range(max_n - 1, -1, -1):
        inv_fact[i] = (inv_fact[i + 1] * (i + 1)) % MOD
    
    # Function to compute C(n, r)
    def combination(n, r):
        if r < 0 or r > n:
            return 0
        return (fact[n] * inv_fact[r] % MOD) * inv_fact[n - r] % MOD
    
    # Calculate answer
    result = 0
    min_count = min(open_count, close_count)
    
    for k in range(1, min_count + 1):
        ways_open = combination(open_count, k)
        ways_close = combination(close_count, k)
        total_ways = (ways_open * ways_close) % MOD
        result = (result + total_ways) % MOD
    
    print(result)

solve()
```
